docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-D1-off-r3.txt:1214:docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-D1-off-r1.txt:696:docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-B1-on.txt:10112:src/entities/docs-vault/data/content.json-16-  "FOUNDATIONS": "# FOUNDATIONS — what grounds ontology-atlas\n\n> This document ties the product to **public, citable academic/standard/craft references** rather than *feelings*.\n> A collection of grounds to prevent \"building without even knowing what ontology is\" — what already exists, and where/how we make *our own thing* differently on top of it.\n>\n> **Citation discipline:** All references cited here are public papers / W3C standards / public books / public blogs.\n> The initial set was re-verified with original text and official metadata on 2026-06-01, and the ontology-construction reinforcement set on 2026-08-09. Links/citations are provided, but the body text is not copied. When adding new references, maintain public, citable, and directly verified standards, and do not cite materials read only via search results.\n\nThis is a living document. When we make a design or feature decision, we should be able to point\nat *which* of these it descends from — or argue explicitly why we diverge.\n\n---\n\n## 1. What an ontology actually is\n\nThe word is older than software (Aristotle's *categories of being*), but our usage is the\n**information-science / knowledge-representation** one.\n\n- **Gruber (1993)** gave the most-cited definition: *\"an ontology is an explicit specification of a\n  conceptualization\"* — a representational vocabulary (classes, relations, functions, objects) for a\n  shared domain, designed so knowledge can be **shared and reused** across AI systems.\n  → Our vault's markdown frontmatter *is* \"an explicit specification of a conceptualization\" of a\n  codebase. The exact Atlas application model — five authorable kinds, one reserved reader kind,\n  relation support layers, `is_a` test, and inference boundary — lives only in\n  [`ONTOLOGY-ATLAS-SPEC.md` §2](ONTOLOGY-ATLAS-SPEC.md#2-the-five-authorable-node-kinds-and-reserved-reader-kind).\n  The name is earned by that shared meaning model, not by pretending the files implement a standard\n  they do not.\n- **Studer, Benjamins & Fensel (1998)** sharpened it to the canonical four-part definition:\n  *\"a **formal, explicit specification** of a **shared conceptualization**.\"* Word by word:\n  *conceptualization* = an abstract model of phenomena; *explicit* = concept types and constraints\n  are defined, not implied; *formal* requires a representation with defined semantics, not merely text\n  that a parser can read; *shared* = held in common rather than private.\n  → Atlas is explicit, shared, and machine-parseable. Its current Markdown schema does **not** claim\n  model-theoretic semantics, logical completeness, or a DL reasoner, so “machine-readable = formal” is\n  not our claim. This distinction is a trust boundary, not a downgrade.\n- **Noy & McGuinness, \"Ontology Development 101\" (Stanford, 2001)** is the practical methodology:\n  define classes + a hierarchy, define properties (slots) + constraints (facets), then instances —\n  and crucially, *\"ontology development is iterative, there is no single correct ontology, and the\n  right design depends on the intended application and anticipated extensions.\"*\n  → This is our **grow-the-vault-each-session, agent-maintained, no-one-true-model** stance, verbatim.\n\n### The formality spectrum — and where we deliberately sit\n\nThe W3C Semantic Web stack is the standard reference frame:\n\n- **RDF 1.1 (2014 Recommendation)** — RDF graphs are sets of subject-predicate-object triples whose\n  terms are IRIs, blank nodes, or datatyped literals. RDF 1.2 reached Candidate Recommendation Snapshot\n  on 2026-04-07; that work-in-progress status is not an endorsement or an Atlas compatibility claim.\n  An Atlas edge can be *mapped* to a triple-like statement, but the vault itself is not an RDF\n  serialization and does not inherit RDF identity or entailment semantics.\n- **OWL 2 (2012)** — the **heavyweight** end: classes/properties/individuals with **description-logic\n  reasoning** (consistency checking, classification, inference) under an open-world assumption. Atlas\n  does not adopt OWL vocabulary, class/individual semantics, or inference. Missing Atlas evidence is\n  `unknown`/a visible gap according to our application contract, not an OWL entailment result.\n- **SKOS (2009)** — the **lightweight** end: `skos:Concept` + `broader`/`narrower`/`related`.\n  `skos:broader` names a direct broader link and is not itself transitive; Atlas borrows that caution,\n  not SKOS semantics or conformance.\n- **SHACL 1.0 (2017 Recommendation)** — validates an RDF data graph against a separate shapes graph\n  and returns a validation report; it does not make OWL a closed-world schema language. SHACL 1.2 Core\n  was still a Working Draft on 2026-08-03. Atlas's validator plays an analogous product role over\n  Markdown, but it is an Atlas schema validator, not a SHACL processor.\n\n> **Our position on the spectrum:** a lightweight, application-specific ontology and executable\n> meaning model — more structured than tags, deliberately less expressive than RDF/OWL. Human-and-agent\n> readable Markdown, typed application relations, deterministic validation/query, no reasoner, no\n> backend. “Graph-shaped” describes the implementation; it does not assert standards conformance.\n\n### Construction is a requirements-and-tests lifecycle, not noun extraction\n\nThe literature converges on one discipline Atlas adopts: **start from the decisions the ontology must\nsupport, not from a directory tree or a list of nouns.** Grüninger & Fox make competency questions\nrequirements and benchmarks; NeOn and LOT put requirements, reuse, implementation, publication, and\nmaintenance in one lifecycle; SAMOD turns a motivating scenario, CQs, a glossary, exemplar data, and\nqueries into a regression-carrying test case.\n\nAtlas translates that lineage into an eight-stage, local-first construction contract:\n\n1. **Purpose and authority** — name the product outcome, intended users/decisions, scope, non-goals,\n   domain-language owners, and sources before proposing concepts.\n2. **Requirements as competency questions** — derive CQs from motivating scenarios; give each an\n   audience, expected answer shape, required witness kinds, explicit unknown/refusal behavior, and\n   owner approval. An LLM may draft a CQ, but may not approve its own requirement.\n3. **Evidence inventory and reuse** — separate direct, triangulated, structural, conflicting, and\n   absent evidence; search the existing vault before minting a term. A folder name is not a domain and\n   an import edge is not automatically a business dependency.\n4. **Small conceptual slice** — propose the smallest project→domain→capability→element slice that\n   answers a coherent CQ set. Give every concept an intensional definition, includes/excludes, examples,\n   counterexamples, and uncertainty. Do not expand the schema to fill a template.\n5. **Semantic and structural tests** — reject circular definitions, duplicate siblings, category\n   mistakes, dangling references, predicate-direction mistakes, and unsupported `is_a`. Same domain,\n   naming similarity, or folder nesting alone never proves subsumption.\n6. **Functional and pragmatic tests** — replay CQs against exemplar facts and expected answers, then\n   test whether a source-hidden person/agent can make the intended decision with bounded calls and\n   supported claims. A valid file is not necessarily a useful ontology.\n7. **Human acceptance and provenance** — persist only the accepted write plan; keep source spans,\n   graph/source digests, rejected/partial CQs, and the responsible human decision visible. Receipts are\n   provenance, not truth certificates.\n8. **Regression and evolution** — after each accepted change, rerun prior CQs, graph/schema validation,\n   source currentness, impact, and handoff checks. Git records change; the ontology still has to explain\n   why the meaning changed and which requirement authorized it.\n\nRecent LLM evidence makes the human-sovereign boundary stronger, not weaker. LLM-generated ontology\ndrafts can outperform novices in bounded studies, but quality remains variable; one 2025 CQ pipeline\nreported only about a quarter of outputs passing scope/relevance and fewer than half as unproblematic.\nThe 2026 IDEA2 workflow therefore separates LLM elicitation from domain-expert validation and records\nthe full CQ revision provenance. Atlas follows that separation: **agents accelerate elicitation,\nconstruction, and repair; people own meaning and accepted requirements; an independent evaluator owns\nqualification.**\n\n### Quality is a vector; no green total may hide a red dimension\n\nOQuaRE treats an ontology both as a software artifact and as a tool that must be useful. Atlas keeps\nthat separation and does not reduce construction quality to node count, fan-out, compiler health, or a\nsingle confidence score.\n\n| Dimension | Atlas question | Required evidence | Fail-closed result |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Semantic correctness | Are kind, boundary, and predicate meanings defensible? | definitions, includes/excludes, examples/counterexamples, relation rationale | reject or `review_required` |\n| Structural conformance | Does the Markdown graph satisfy its declared schema? | parser/compiler/validator results, resolved references | block invalid writes/finalization |\n| Functional adequacy | Can it answer the approved CQs? | expected answer + quantified witness coverage | `partial` / `visible-gap` |\n| Evidence and provenance | Can each business claim be traced to current sources and an authority? | citations, source spans/digests, approval and receipt lineage | unsupported / stale |\n| Pragmatic usefulness | Can each audience make the intended decision without hidden source access? | source-hidden task result, claim ledger, time/calls | unknown / not-qualified |\n| Maintainability | Can a change be reviewed and prior meaning retested? | git diff, impact path, CQ regression, source-currentness check | maintenance action required |\n| Interoperability honesty | Are exports and standards claims limited to what is implemented? | declared format/profile and round-trip contract | no RDF/OWL/SHACL conformance claim |\n\nStructural metrics and automated judges are diagnostic signals, not acceptance authorities. OOPS! and\nOntoClean can expose modeling pitfalls; LLM graph judges can help triage noisy extraction; none replaces\ndomain requirements, exact witnesses, counterexamples, or independent task-level evaluation.\n\nThe re-executable evaluator packet, categorical verdict, privacy boundary, and representative fixture\nare specified in [Ontology Construction Qualification v1](ONTOLOGY-CONSTRUCTION-QUALIFICATION.md).\n\nFor Atlas, the conservative direct-subsumption test is executable prose, not a reasoner claim: both\nendpoints have the same `domain`/`capability`/`element` kind, every valid narrower example satisfies the\nbroader definition, the distinction is not synonymy or membership/part/dependency/sequence, and no known\naccepted concept fits between them. The normative rule and current `broader` write boundary live in the\nsame [meta-model contract](ONTOLOGY-ATLAS-SPEC.md#22-direct-is_a--broader-test).\n\n---\n\n## 2. Agent memory & LLM × knowledge-graph (the live field, 2023–2026)\n\nOur wedge — *\"the AI agent forgets the codebase every session; this is git-native ontology memory it\nmaintains and queries\"* — sits inside an active, recognized research lineage. We are **not** inventing\nthe category; we are taking a specific, opinionated position in it.\n\n**The \"LLM forgets; give it external memory\" premise:**\n\n- **MemGPT (Packer et al., 2023)** — \"virtual context management\": the model pages information between\n  a fixed context window (fast memory) and external stores (slow memory) via self-issued function\n  calls, simulating unbounded context. → We are the **codebase-specific, structured & human-readable\n  version of MemGPT's \"slow memory\"**: the agent pages the repo's mental model in/out through a runtime-advertised MCP surface\n  instead of opaque archival storage.\n- **\"A Survey on the Memory Mechanism of LLM-based Agents\" (Zhang et al., ACM TOIS, 2024)** — the\n  peer-reviewed taxonomy of memory *sources / forms / operations* and *evaluation*. → Lets us name our\n  choices precisely: **structured (KG) over textual** memory, **write-on-task-completion** operations,\n  **human-readable + git-versioned** storage, **codebase** domain. Safest single category-overview citation.\n\n**Memory as a maintained knowledge graph (the closest analogues):**\n\n- **Zep / Graphiti (Rasmussen et al., 2025)** — agent memory as a **temporal knowledge graph** with a\n  bi-temporal model (event time + ingestion time). The single closest published analogue to us: both\n  treat long-term agent memory as an explicit, maintained graph of typed entities + relations, not a\n  vector blob. → **Where we differ:** we get temporality *for free* from **git history + markdown\n  diffs** rather than a dual-timeline DB; our domain is the **codebase**, not conversation; our store\n  is **plain files in the repo**, not a graph DB.\n- **Mem0 (Chhikara et al., ECAI 2025)** — production memory with a plain-extraction mode *and* a\n  graph mode (`Mem0g`); emphasizes token-cost / latency savings from retrieving a small relevant slice\n  instead of full context. → Mirrors our choice to make **the graph the primary artifact** (markdown\n  frontmatter *is* the graph), and supplies our pragmatic argument: a maintained ontology lets the\n  agent fetch a small relevant subgraph instead of re-reading the whole repo each session.\n\n**LLM × KG more broadly:**\n\n- **Pan et al., \"Unifying LLMs and Knowledge Graphs: A Roadmap\" (IEEE TKDE, 2024)** — the canonical\n  framing of three patterns: KG-enhanced LLMs, **LLM-augmented KGs** (the LLM does construction /\n  completion / QA), and **synergized** LLM+KG as equals. KGs are positioned as *explicit, interpretable,\n  editable* stores that compensate for the LLM black box. → This *is* our core thesis. Our vault loop is\n  \"LLM-augmented KG + synergized\": the agent both **queries** the graph for grounding and\n  **maintains/extends** it.\n- **GraphRAG (Edge et al., Microsoft, 2024; + `microsoft/graphrag`)** — an LLM derives an entity graph\n  from documents, pre-summarizes communities, and answers *global* questions flat vector-RAG can't.\n  → The flagship example of an LLM **building and querying a graph as a memory/index layer**; we apply\n  the same pattern to a **codebase**. **Where we differ:** GraphRAG produces a generated artifact store;\n  our graph is a **human-editable, diff-reviewable markdown vault** that is the source of truth.\n- **\"LLMs on Graphs: A Comprehensive Survey\" (Jin et al., TKDE, 2023/2024)** — vocabulary for what we\n  are: a **text-attributed graph** (frontmatter nodes + typed edges) an LLM reasons over (as\n  predictor / encoder / aligner). Grounds the claim that LLMs *can* reason over typed-edge graphs, not\n  only prose.\n- **\"LLM-empowered Knowledge Graph Construction: A Survey\" (Bian, 2025, preprint)** — schema-based vs\n  schema-free KG construction, and explicitly names **\"dynamic memory for agentic systems\"** as a\n  direction. → Our per-kind normalized frontmatter schema is the schema-based pole; \"dynamic memory for\n  agentic systems\" is our exact positioning.\n- **\"Towards Agentic RAG with Deep Reasoning: A Survey\" (2025, preprint)** — \"agentic RAG\": an agent\n  loops over an external store to ground multi-step reasoning. → Frames our loop in current terms:\n  RAG-*enhanced reasoning* over a maintained graph, not one-shot retrieval.\n\n---\n\n## 3. The codebase side — code knowledge graphs\n\nThe other half of our lineage: representing **code** as a queryable graph rather than re-parsing it\neach time. The premise that a codebase's meaning is best captured as a graph of typed nodes + typed\nedges is well-established academic and industrial prior art.\n\n- **Code Property Graphs (Yamaguchi et al., IEEE S&P 2014)** — fuse AST + control-flow + program-\n  dependence into one queryable graph; express patterns as graph traversals (found 18 unknown Linux\n  vulnerabilities). → The foundational academic precedent for \"**a codebase's meaning is a unified\n  graph you traverse, not re-derive.**\" We fuse `project/domain/capability/element` + edges into one\n  vault the same way.\n- **Glean (Meta, open-source, 2024)** — schema-defined **typed facts** about code in a queryable fact\n  DB, interrogated with a Datalog-style language; powers navigation across a monorepo. → The closest\n  *industrial* analogue. **Contrast:** Glean = machine-*generated* facts; we = **human + agent-maintained,\n  git-native, markdown-as-source-of-truth.**\n- **SCIP (Sourcegraph, 2022)** — a portable, **human-readable**, language-agnostic code-index protocol\n  with stable string symbol IDs. → Validates our choice of a **portable, human-readable, on-disk**\n  representation (markdown frontmatter; slug-keyed nodes) over an opaque binary index.\n- **CodeQL (GitHub/Semmle)** — \"**treat code as data**\": extract a relational DB of facts, query it to\n  find patterns/variants. → The mainstream embodiment of \"**query your codebase like a database**\" —\n  precisely what we offer an agent via focused MCP graph queries (`find_path`, `find_backlinks`, …)\n  instead of\n  re-reading files.\n- **tree-sitter (Brunsfeld et al.)** — incremental parsing → concrete syntax trees with an\n  S-expression query system; the substrate beneath most code indexers (and our own CodeGraph index).\n  → The **structural** layer (\"what symbols exist, how they nest\"). We layer the **meaning** layer\n  (domains, capabilities, evidence, impact) *on top* — that separation is the point.\n\n> **The gap we fill:** CPG / Glean / CodeQL / tree-sitter answer *structural* questions\n> (what calls what, what's defined where) — machine-derived, exhaustive, regenerated. We hold the\n> **human-and-agent-meaningful** layer: *why* this exists, *which capability* it serves, *what breaks*\n> if it changes — curated (not exhaustive), maintained by the developer + agent together, in git.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Design lineage — restraint as craft, cited\n\nOur \"Linear-restrained\" design language (`docs/DESIGN-SYSTEM.md`, `.claude/rules/design.md`) is an\napplied reading of public, citable design thinking — not arbitrary taste.\n\n- **Dieter Rams, \"Ten Principles for Good Design\" (Vitsœ)** — *unobtrusive, honest, long-lasting,\n  thorough to the last detail,* and *\"as little design as possible\"* (\"Less, but better\").\n  → The rationale for neutral greys + a single indigo and the bans on glow/neon/gradients/glassmorphism.\n  Our whole forbidden-pattern list is applied Rams.\n- **Edward Tufte, *The Visual Display of Quantitative Information*** — **graphical integrity**\n  (representation proportional to the quantities) and **direct labelling** (a legend means the mark\n  cannot explain itself). → Grounds honest, proportional rendering

There is no second right dock and no\n  separate review route.\n- One edit handles **one relation**. The user chooses type, target, and rationale,\n  or removes that existing relation from the same review path;\n  `depends_on` requires a rationale. Invalid `is_a` and containment target kinds\n  are filtered before selection.\n- The real map draws a dashed directional preview between the live endpoint\n  coordinates. This overlay never enters the force graph, so it cannot pull nodes\n  or change graph statistics. A density-hidden target is temporarily rendered at\n  its real coordinate and label. The responsive\n  contract keeps at least 480px of map between left chrome and editor from 1024px\n  upward; below `lg`, the editor is the single centered sheet above the tab bar.\n- New concept creation uses `workbench=create`. It no longer calls `createDoc`\n  from the first button press: the generated UID, slug, kind, display labels,\n  domain, and authorship fields are shown in `OntologyChangeReview`, and only\n  \"Confirm and write\" creates the file.\n- ACP keeps read tools frictionless. The\n  review accordion selects one exact item at a time for the map preview, while\n  allow/reject applies honestly to the whole batch; the first item is never used\n  as a stand-in for hidden rows.\n- `/ontology/studio` and `/ontology/edit` remain only as compatibility addresses.\n  `node/mode/edit/via/review` are translated to `p/workbench/edit` on `/topology`.\n\n<details>\n<summary>Retired Compass Stage details (historical reference)</summary>\n\nThe following behavior described the removed Studio UI. It is retained only to\nexplain old screenshots and decisions; none of it is a current destination.\n\n- This is the **write screen** that fills in a concept's description and relationships. It places the node currently being edited large and centered on the screen, fixing the direction for each relationship type: up = parent concept (is_a), down = what this concept contains (contains), right = what this concept depends on (depends), left = similar concepts (relates). Directions must always remain constant so users do not need to re-read them every time. It opens from the \"Workshop\" in the left vertical menu. **There is only one screen, differing only in how much is filled, with no mode-selection tabs.**\n- **Enhancing existing nodes**: Opens an existing node (specified via a `?node=<id>` link; if unspecified, it automatically selects the capability with the most relationships) to fill in missing relationships. Already-filled relationships are drawn as indigo solid lines with small cards at their ends, while still-empty relationships are drawn as **empty spaces** with dashed lines (these are empty cells with only lines, not decorative icons). A \"Start filling here\" prompt is attached to only one of them.\n- **Creating new nodes (`?mode=create`)**: Opens the same screen entirely blank — a draft card for kind/name/domain/definition and empty relationship slots in four directions. Before saving, it separately notifies the user of \"1 new node, N relationships.\" If the name is similar to an existing node, the user can choose between \"Open existing node\" and \"Create anyway.\" However, if the kind and name match so closely that the file slug also overlaps, only \"Open existing node\" remains available; saving, save-previews, and change-previews are all blocked. The name input field is linked to this warning so screen readers announce it as well. Results are previewed in real-time while typing.\n- **Files change, not just the screen**: Filling an empty relationship slot writes directly to the frontmatter relationship list of the actual `.md` file (`localVault.updateFrontmatter`). In read-only vaults (e.g., samples), it instead copies a **bundle of MCP commands** for the AI agent to the clipboard. The target to connect with is selected from the list that appears immediately at that spot or created via \"Create new.\"\n- **Concepts without their own files are asked first**: Many concepts in the vault exist only because other documents listed their names in relationship lists, meaning they lack their own `.md` file (in this repository's vault, 198 out of 294). Since relationships are stored within a concept's file, one must create a file first to link relationships to such concepts. Because creating new files on the user's disk is something the user has never explicitly requested, **the system shows the file path to be created and asks for confirmation once** at the moment of saving. If cancelled, no files change (written content remains as a draft). If confirmed, a document containing both the relationships and the **path already pointed to by existing documents** is generated in a single write operation. If the kind cannot be determined, it does not arbitrarily decide but lets the user choose. In read-only vaults, it provides an MCP command bundle including `add_concept`.\n- **Enabled actual saving of parent concepts (is_a)**: \"What kind of thing is this node a kind of?\" was the most frequently empty item in the vault. Therefore, we added the `broader` key (the name used in SKOS standards) to the frontmatter and actually added it to graph calculations, schemas (mcp/cli), and gates so all components recognize this key. When filled, the dashed line becomes a solid line.\n- **How to show how much is filled**: Displayed via the borders of the four sides of the central card (dashed for empty sides, solid for filled sides). Below that is an easy-to-understand explanation (\"Filled 2 out of 4\"), and in the top-left corner is a small compass icon indicating what to do next. Game-style displays like percentage pie charts, levels, or grades are not used.\n- **Separated two seemingly similar questions (2026-07-28)**: The top button row is for selecting the node's **kind**, and the relationship slot in the up (↑) direction is for recording the **`broader` relationship**, i.e., \"is a sub-concept of which concept.\" These are different facts. Therefore, the button row has a visible single-word label \"Kind / Kind\" connected via `aria-labelledby`. The English question in the upper relationship slot is `What is this node a kind of?` — the previous phrase `What kind of thing is this node?` was corrected because it literally read as asking \"the kind of this node.\"\n- **Does not open below 1024px width and explains why (2026-07-28)**: This screen consists of a fixed-width card and relationship slots placed around its perimeter, making it unworkable on narrow screens (the installed app has a minimum width of `minWidth 1040`, so such widths do not appear at all, and the Workshop is excluded from mobile bottom tab bars). Links entering in `<lg` width from three sources (\"Edit Relationships\" in node details, Insights, Document frontmatter) now receive a single card explaining **why** (1024px width is required and it opens immediately when the window is widened) and **where to go** (Map · Desktop app). The Workshop screen itself is not drawn, nor does the first-visit guide appear on it — because introducing a non-existent screen would be a lie.\n- **Design**: Follows the same rules as the rest of the app — achromatic + one indigo + using only `--color-*` tokens. Amber (orange) is used solely as a signal for \"places that should naturally be filled but are empty.\" **Glow · gradients · gems · particles · gold are prohibited** (the previously existing game-style exception was abolished on 2026-07-24). The only motion is a change in opacity and color over 200ms when filling a relationship slot, and even that stops under the `prefers-reduced-motion` setting. All screen text uses plain language (\"What kind of thing is this node?\").\n\n</details>\n\n### `/ontology/edit` and `/ontology/studio` — compatibility redirects\n\nBoth old addresses now use `OntologyEditRedirectPage` to translate legacy\n`?node=`, `?mode=create`, and `?edit=` values into `/topology` workbench state.\nThe routes remain in the static export so old bookmarks do not 404; neither is\na navigation destination or a product screen.\n\n\n---\n\n### `/projects` — Project list (rebuilt 2026-07-18)\n\nRebuilt against RATIO-SYSTEM (1600px shared container). Creating asks for one thing — make a project — so the screen asks for exactly the four fields that make one, and nothing else is on top of them.\n\n- **Four essential fields, first screen, no scroll** — name · category · status · short description. Measured at 1512×950: name at y=292, category/status at y=395, description at y=472, primary action at y=698; the whole screen fits without scrolling (also verified at 1024 and 768).\n- **The document address (slug) is a caption, not a field.** It is derived from the name; \"Set it myself\" opens the real input inline (validation errors targeting it open it automatically).\n- **Everything else is folded into \"Fill in more\"** — nameEn, detail, tags, stack, links, dependencies, dates, owner, icon, progress, hub flag. The user opens it; a validation error inside it opens it too.\n- **Actions come after the form** — Create & continue / Create & return / Cancel. The old top save cluster is gone in create mode (it let you press \"create\" before seeing a single input); edit mode keeps its sticky bar.\n- **One place teaches.** The four teaching surfaces that used to stack above the form (two tip cards on the page, one header help line, one \"1-minute tip\" disclosure) all said the same sentence and pushed the actual fields off screen. They are replaced by one subtitle line directly above the fields, plus per-field captions.\n- `ProjectQuickCreatePanel` still exists as a component but is no longer surfaced from `/projects` (2026-07-18); this full form remains the canonical create path.\n\n### `/project/fallback` — Static-export fallback\n\nUsed when a non-existent slug is hit in static export. Unpublished: a single \"not out yet\" sentence, no placeholder facts\n- **Windows** — unpublished: one honest beta-pending line. Published: one unsigned x64 NSIS installer CTA derived from its real release URL and byte size, with a warning immediately before it; GitHub Release carries the sibling SHA-256 asset. Native CI proves build/scan/install/launch/MCP behavior; Windows 11 SmartScreen UI remains unverified\n- Source of truth: `src/views/download/model/macos-release.generated.ts`, written by `pnpm download:release-facts` from the real GitHub Release\n\n#### \"Includes\" cards (3, sm+)\n- Topology map · MCP server (tool count) · CLI (command count)\n\n#### Install steps (4, numbered 01–04, sm+ 2-col grid)\n\n#### Trust panel + changelog preview (2-col on lg+)\n- **Trust panel** — \"Developer ID signed\" / \"Notarized by Apple\" / \"checksums published\" stated as facts about published builds (never as a gate that \"requires\" them) + a `spctl --assess --type open --context context:primary-signature ...` verify command built from the current version's DMG filename + the policy note that a build failing a gate is never published\n- **Changelog preview** (`CHANGELOG_PREVIEW_ENTRIES`, sourced from `docs/CHANGELOG.md`) — version + a handful of recent entry titles + \"as of DATE\" caption\n\n#### GitHub row + release-gate note + footer\n- GitHub repo link row · the hosted-site scope note (the website never opens or edits vault folders) · footer (license / GitHub / stack)\n\n---\n\n### `/git` — Record (record destination, workbench redesign 2026-07-27)\n\n**One sentence on what this screen does**: Verify what concept I changed and decide whether to leave it in one git commit. Therefore, the most prominent things on the screen are **the list of changed concepts and the \"Leave\" button** pair; everything else is either evidence for that judgment or merely the top/bottom borders of the screen.\n\nThe screen layout splits into two stages. First, **is this screen even in a state to do work** (`data-stage`) — if the browser can't run git or the vault hasn't been selected yet, it stops here. If it can work, it then splits by **whether there is something to decide now** (`data-shape`).\n\n#### State where work cannot start yet (`web` · `no-vault` · `not-initialized` · `loading` · `error`)\n- These states are all drawn in **the same size, same position** (`--git-setup-measure`\n  one 520px cell, centered on screen). If the width changes per stage, users feel like they're jumping to different pages every time\n- Shows what to do in one line: Open app → select folder → start recording.\n  Registering a remote repository is optional so it's not included in this line. No additional menus or decorative links here\n- In the browser, `Get App` is the primary button, and copying CLI commands for terminal use is secondary\n\n#### State with uncommitted changes (`decide`)\n- Left: One line of status totals at the top, then **file rows grouped by kind**\n  (status symbols `+ ~ − →` · folder names larger · how many lines added/removed). Clicking a row shows changed lines from that document on the right\n- Non-concept files (`.gitignore`, etc.) are **collapsed by default** — they go into the commit but aren't things humans need to judge. The number of collapsed lines is shown numerically so it's not hidden\n- Bottom fixed bar: Indigo-filled `Leave N items` button → confirmation step (preview of the actual commit title line + whether to push remotely, default off). Text explaining what is being recorded is also here — because files are actually written here\n- Right: Area showing evidence — `Changed lines` (file-by-file +/- lines with git internal notation removed) and `Previous steps` (previous commits). **Drawn only when there is content to show**\n- The width for splitting into 2 columns is `xl` (1280). Making it 2 columns at 1024 compresses the list and cuts off concept names\n\n#### State with nothing to leave (`recall`)\n- Does not split into 2 columns. A single-screen view where **the previous commit list is the body** (`--git-single-measure`)\n- What's in one commit line: how recent · simple summary (`Added 3 · Modified 2`) · author · short hash. Expanding shows full hash · ISO format time · **original commit title** (record needed for later tracking)\n- The primary button position remains inactive (`All left`). If the button disappears entirely based on state, users have to figure out what to press next every time\n\n#### Only simple language on screen\n- Commit titles automatically created by Atlas (`ontology snapshot: +3 concepts, …`) are converted to human language when shown on screen. Conversely, manually written commits or those made by other tools are left as-is since the original text is already human language (`describeSnapshotSubject`)\n- Git's internal notation (`diff --git` · `index <sha>..<sha>` ·\n  `@@ -a,b +c,d @@`) is not exposed on screen. However, a dashed line is **left** for skipped sections — hiding the fact that it was skipped makes that diff a lie\n\n#### Nothing is written until the user clicks\nWhen the screen first opens, only read-only tools are called (`git_status` / `git_diff` / `git_history`). Tools that change something (`git_init` · `git_set_remote` · `git_snapshot`) are executed only when the user presses their button (`onClick`).\n\n### `/agents` — Agent (new 2026-08-20, catalog 90)\n\n**One sentence on what this screen does**: **Get · install · attach · fix · and start conversation with** the AI coding tool on this computer.\n\n- **List** — Tools actually verified on this device are shown first, others are collapsed.\n- **Connection check** — Re-evaluate eight steps (does tool exist · can it launch · does it ask outside folder · is downloaded item intact · app-side settings · credential link · old login records · login). **Fixable things are fixed right there.** For unfixable ones, write what the human needs to do.\n- **App-specific installation** — Downloads Node and tools only inside the app folder. Fixes versions, and after downloading Node, **compares hashes** (if mismatched, delete and stop). Shows the original text before executing anything. Progress and completion remain on screen — even if you close and reopen the window.\n- **Reconnection** — Deletes only what the app created and recreates it. This is not \"logout\": this app has no app-side login, and links to the login the user did in the terminal, using it as-is.\n\n**Why it came out of settings**: Settings is **where you choose values**, and this is **an operational task with progress state**. A modal blocks the background and owns Esc, preventing you from seeing the map while receiving 52MB. **API Keys and workspaces remain in settings** — the former has a \"Path Freezing\" decision on 2026-08-16 (promoting destination is itself an emphasis), and the latter's axis answered by vault is different.\n\n**On the web**: The screen still appears, but states why it can't do what the browser can't (launching programs on this computer) along with the reason. It's not \"Connection unavailable\" — MCP is **attached to the folder**, not the screen, so web users are also connected (catalog 2026-08-01).\n\n## 3. MCP server (current runtime inventory)\n\nAI agents read/write the same vault as humans. Two ways to get the server running, and only two:\n\n| Channel | How the agent starts it | What the user does |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Installed desktop app** (primary; macOS 2026-07-27, Windows beta 2026-08-01) | The app ships a compiled MCP server inside its own bundle (`Ontology Atlas.app/Contents/MacOS/ontology-atlas-mcp` on macOS, `ontology-atlas-mcp.exe` beside the Windows executable). The agent client spawns that binary directly, so it keeps serving while the app is closed. | Open the vault folder in the app and press **Connect agent**. The app writes `.mcp.json` / `.codex/config.toml` with the bundled binary's absolute path and the vault's real path already filled in — no terminal, no Node, no install step. |\n| **Source checkout** (fallback) | `node <checkout>/mcp/src/index.js` with `OATLAS_VAULT` set. | Clone the repo, then either paste the config or let `node <checkout>/cli/src/index.mjs init` / `agent-setup --write` write it. |\n\nnpm publishing is retired (`docs/DECISIONS.md`, 2026-07-27) — there is no `npx` channel.\n\n**Connecting a project to its code (2026-08-04).** `connect_project_source`\n(CLI `connect-source`) binds one project node to the local folder holding the\ncode it describes, measures it, and writes the source receipt that\n`agent_brief` reports. Omit the folder and it infers one — the git repository\nenclosing the vault, otherwise the nearest ancestor carrying a project\nmanifest — then tells you how many of the ontology's declared `path:` claims\nactually exist inside it before anything is written. `confirm: true` binds;\n`disconnect_project_source` (CLI `disconnect-source`) undoes it. Until this\nlanded, the app could say \"no code folder is connected\" and name\n`connect_source` as the next action while nothing outside the macOS folder\npicker could perform it.\n\n**R14 — workflow automation** (Claude Code + Codex):\n\n| Trigger | What | Where |\n|---|---|---|\n| **SessionStart hook** (implicit) | Compact vault census auto-injected into agent context on session start: total nodes, kind distribution, and only an actionable drift warning when needed. The hook deliberately avoids domains, hub lists, and full node tables to keep token use low. | `.claude/hooks/inject-ontology-summary.sh` / `.codex/hooks/inject-ontology-summary.sh` — silent in repos without a vault |\n| **Explicit live activity CLI** | Agents or humans can still publish `.ontology-atlas/agent-activity.json` through `ontology-atlas agent-activity` when a handoff needs it. The automatic PreToolUse heartbeat hooks were removed during the token-budget pass; routine shell commands no longer update the sidecar implicitly. | `cli/src/commands/agent-activity.mjs` · `src/features/docs-vault-local/model/agent-activity-status.ts` |\n| **`/ontology-bootstrap` skill** (cold start) | Empty vault → evidence-earned first graph. `analyze_repo_structure` side-effect-zero → exact non-writing review plan → maker-independent CQ/source-hidden qualification whose claims bind to that plan with `proposalRefs` → user accepts that digest and every visible gap → only the released unchanged rows reach batch writers → validate/compile/source-connect/finalize. Missing evidence, proposal coverage, or independent evaluation stops without writes. the local vault picker moved to **App Settings → Workspace**), matching installed-CLI graph runbook (`validate` → `workspace-brief` → `agent-brief --prompt` → `agent-brief --graph-db-pack` → `agent-brief --verify-fallbacks` → `cycles` → `growth` → `maintenance` → `hubs --plan` → `hubs` → `mcp-verify`), a separate one-click automation gate (`agent-brief --verify-fallbacks --json --fallback-timeout-ms 15000 --fallback-slow-ms 5000 --fallback-concurrency 4`) with visible command preview, the visible first-contact proof contract (`config_state` → `mcp_verify` → `json_gate` → `graph_briefs`), a separate codebase-root `agent-setup` repair command copy button, codebase-root `.mcp.json.example` template, codebase-root Codex `.codex/config.toml` template, and a one-line `codex mcp add ...` command for users who prefer Codex CLI registration;
