Each fallback command has a 15s default timeout, configurable with `--fallback-timeout-ms N` or `OATLAS_AGENT_FALLBACK_TIMEOUT_MS=N`, and timeout rows report `timedOut:true` for fail-closed setup automation. Passing-but-slow rows are counted under `slow`, marked with `slow:true`, and summarized by `performanceOk:false` when they exceed the 5s default `slowThresholdMs`, tunable with `--fallback-slow-ms N` or `OATLAS_AGENT_FALLBACK_SLOW_MS=N`; fallback concurrency defaults to 4 and is tunable with `--fallback-concurrency N` or `OATLAS_AGENT_FALLBACK_CONCURRENCY=N`, so automation can distinguish broken setup from local graph latency drift without making the setup gate unnecessarily slow. Root-level CLI init writes matching cwd configs for codebase-root sessions; a repeated init rebinds those root-local Atlas entries to the newly requested active vault and still requires a client restart plus `connection_info` proof. | `cli/src/index.mjs` · `src/features/docs-vault-local/lib/ontology-starter.ts` · `src/features/docs-vault-local/model/use-local-vault.ts` · `src/widgets/app-settings-menu/ui/VaultAgentSetupPanel.tsx` · `src/widgets/app-settings-menu/ui/AppSettingsMenu.tsx` · `src/views/docs-vault/ui/DocsVaultPage.tsx` |\n| **10-minute memory loop smoke** | Fresh repo `init -> bootstrap -> validate -> workspace_brief -> agent_brief -> node_profile -> sync proposal` path is executable as a release-readiness gate, including git diff alignment before any side-effecting sync write. | `scripts/smoke-memory-loop.mjs` · `pnpm smoke:memory-loop` |\n| **`mcp__ontology-atlas__*` `instructions` field** (R13 v0.7.1) | Server's initialize response carries kind hierarchy, first-time workflow, write safety patterns — every connecting agent gets the discipline without trial-and-error | `mcp/src/index.js` |\n| **`.ontology-atlasignore`** (R+) | Vault-root gitignore-style file. **It does not exclude any file from the vault** — the name invites that reading, but nothing is hidden from `validate`, the graph, or search. Patterns match `materialize_external_element` refs in `growth_plan` / `maintenance_plan` and skip *those suggestions*. Intentional external code (e.g. `src/**`, `cli/**`) stops surfacing as noise. `externalElementRefsIgnored` count exposed for transparency | `mcp/src/ontology-atlas-ignore.mjs` (this vault has no ignore file — it needs none) |\n\nR14 also unified `add_concept` / CLI `add` / CLI `import` to a single per-kind frontmatter schema (`mcp/src/schema.mjs` ↔ `cli/src/lib/schema.mjs`) — three entry points, one shape.\n\n#### Prose / wiki absorption\n\nThree ingress paths land the same vault, differing only by input source and\napproval granularity — none of them let an agent write unreviewed nodes:\n\n| Input | Path | Approval unit |\n|---|---|---|\n| Pasted prose (meeting note / PR / RFC paragraph) | `/ontology-extract` skill — `find_evidence` + `similar_nodes` cross-check → candidate table | Per candidate node/edge |\n| Local CLAUDE.md/AGENTS.md-style file | `absorb_document` MCP tool / CLI `ontology-atlas absorb` — splits by `##` section, classifies policy vs. architecture, Tier 1 injection filter. MCP canonical paths outside `repoRoot` (including symlink escapes) need explicit `allowOutsideRepo:true` after preview | Per section (dry-run first, `confirm:true` to write) |\n| Confluence/Notion/wiki page, via a third-party wiki MCP the user already registered (e.g. Atlassian's official Confluence MCP) | `/ontology-absorb-confluence` skill — that MCP reads the page (read-only), the returned markdown is fed into the same `absorb_document` pipeline, and the source page URL is cited in each landed node's body | Per section (dry-run first, `confirm:true` to write) |\n\nThe wiki path works identically for on-premise Confluence/wiki instances — the\nabsorption tool only ever reads a local markdown file; whatever gets it there\n(cloud MCP, on-prem export, `curl` to a file) is out of scope for this repo.\nThis project does not ship a Confluence integration; it ships an absorption\npipeline that a second, user-owned MCP can feed.\n\n### Interop — export to a standard graph format\n\nData flows *out* the same way it flows in: as portable files, no backend. The\nCLI `ontology-atlas export [vault] --format jsonld|graphml|json` compiles the\nvault (the deterministic `compile_ontology` artifact) and writes a standard\ninterchange format to stdout (status to stderr, so it pipes cleanly):\n\n- `jsonld` — RDF 1.1 JSON-LD; loads in rdflib / Protégé / any triplestore\n  (`g.parse('atlas.jsonld', format='json-ld')`).\n- `graphml` — XML graph; opens in Gephi / Cytoscape, loads via NetworkX\n  (`nx.read_graphml`) or Neo4j APOC (`apoc.import.graphml`).\n- `json` — the raw compile artifact unchanged (nodes/edges/`graphHash`).\n\nThe CLI `export` command emits JSON-LD/GraphML from this *same* serializer, kept\nbyte-identical by a contract test. (The web ERD builder that also consumed it was\nretired 2026-07-24 with the rest of `/ontology/edit`.) Node identity is the stable\n`urn:uuid:<uid>` (both the JSON-LD `@id` and the GraphML node id), while slug is\nexported as the readable current address. Contract: **an export is a snapshot**,\nthe compiler `graphHash` is its **version**, rename/reclassify preserve the URN,\nmerge preserves the survivor URN and absorbs source identities, and\nexternal/dangling refs are omitted\n(never phantom nodes). Full loading recipes live in `mcp/README.md` → *Interop*.\nRead-only MCP registration for external read consumers: set `OATLAS_READ_ONLY=1`\n(`tools/list` exposes only read tools; write calls are rejected).\n\nStaying file-only here is deliberate and matches the Obsidian precedent\n(files + offline core, servers behind an opt-in localhost plugin). A live HTTP\ntransport is out of scope until two concrete external-tool requests prove that\nfile export + the local stdio MCP genuinely can't serve them.\n\n**R14 — vault live updates** (`/topology` + all pages):\n\n- **Adaptive polling** (visible-only) — `useLocalVault` fingerprint check while the tab is visible; bursts to ~1.5s right after a detected change and decays to ~5s when idle, so agent / CLI writes surface fast without idle churn (generation-token poller avoids orphaned timers across hide/show)\n- **Graph diff pulse** — newly appearing slugs amber-pulse for 5s on `/topology`\n- **Toasts** — `Added: <slug>` (info) / `Edited: <slug>` (success, mtime change) on every page\n- **Save-conflict guard** — if a file changed on disk between read and write, `/docs` editor save surfaces a localized conflict notice and keeps the buffer dirty instead of silently overwriting unsaved edits\n- Effect: When files are edited via IDE · AI agent · CLI, the graph updates and toasts appear within ~1.5–5s without the user needing to click the web tab again.\n\n#### Read tools (19)\n1. **connection_info** — active vault/repo roots plus the actually advertised `readOnly`, `toolCount`, `toolNames`, and `toolsetHash`; explicit `OATLAS_REPO_ROOT` wins, otherwise repo root is auto-discovered from the active vault's Git top-level before falling back to process cwd\n2. **git_status** — vault-scoped working-tree state and risk; no writes or remote transport\n3. **git_history** `{ limit? }` — newest-first commits that touched the active vault pathspec only (default 20, max 100), with `limited` / `hasMore`, shallow-repository state, and `historyComplete` so truncated evidence is not mistaken for complete history\n4. **list_concepts** `{ kind?, domain?, since?, summary?, offset?, limit? }` — every node as `{uid, slug, …}`, optional filters, deterministic slug ordering, mtime, summary preview, and explicit `{returned, limited, pagination:{offset,limit,total,returned,hasMore,nextOffset}}` metadata for lossless large-vault traversal\n5. **get_concept** exactly one of `{ slug }` or `{ uid }` — full detail with both identities, frontmatter, prose, neighbors, edges, and `mtime`; UID lookup survives rename and never falls back to fuzzy matching\n6. **get_concepts** exactly one of `{ slugs }` or `{ uids }` — batch read (max 50), order-preserving partial results with both identities and per-node warnings\n7. **find_evidence** `{ title }` — partial-match across title / capabilities / elements / body; each match carries `{uid, slug}`, `domain`, `mtime`, and prose excerpt\n8. **find_backlinks** `{ slug }` — every referencing node as `{uid, slug, …}` (frontmatter arrays + wikilinks/markdown)\n9. **find_neighbors** `{ slug, direction?, types?, includeNodes?, limit? }` — one-hop local graph around a node, with canonical incoming/outgoing `edges[]` and `{uid, slug}` neighbor summaries (`includeNodes` defaults true, `limit` defaults 100/max 500); public relation type aliases like `depends_on` are normalized to stored graph keys\n10. **find_path** `{ from, to, maxHops? }` — shortest undirected BFS across graph frontmatter, including `domains` / `domain` containment (default 5 hops, includes aligned `{uid, slug}` `nodes[]` summaries plus `edges[via]`)\n11. **list_kinds** — vault kind census `{ total, byKind: { capability: N, … } }`\n12. **find_orphans** `{ kind?, excludeKinds? }` — isolated `{uid, slug}` nodes across graph frontmatter, including `domains` / `domain` containment (defaults exclude `project` and `vault-readme`; pass `excludeKinds: []` to include every kind)\n13. **query_concepts** `{ filter, limit? }` — typed filter DSL with AND/OR/NOT on `kind` / `domain` / `slug` / `title` / `has(arrayKey)`; match rows carry `{uid, slug}`\n14. **compile_ontology** `{ includeIndexes?, summary?, nodesLimit?, nodesOffset?, edgesLimit?, edgesOffset? }` — deterministic graph artifact with UID-required `nodes[]`, slug-based `edges[]`, identity indexes (`uidToSlug`, `slugToUid`, `mergedUidToSlug`), graph-array canonicalization actions, semantic `graphHash`, and pagination; invalid identity fails closed\n15. **query_ontology** `{ operation, ... }` — graph-engine query over the compiled artifact (`neighbors`, `path` with aligned `nodes[]`, `all_paths` with per-path `nodes[]` plus `limit` / `searchBudget` / `exhaustive` / `truncatedByBudget` / `totalPathsExact` metadata and `evidence` guidance, `query_plan` with executable run/narrow advice, filter-preserving `suggestedQuery`, and filter-aware `estimate.totalMatches` for `match_nodes` / `match_edges`, `centrality`, `communities`, `similar_nodes`, `explain_relation`, `reachability`, `pattern_walk`, `impact`, `blast_radius`, `subgraph`, `builder_context`, `overview`, `schema`, `facets`, `match_nodes`, `match_edges`, `node_profile`, `domain_profile`, `domain_matrix`, `project_scope`, `project_map`, `relation_check`, `components`, `lineage`, `containment_tree`, `cycles`, `topological_order`, `recommend_relations`, `growth_plan`, `maintenance_plan`, `agent_brief`, `workspace_brief`, `health`) for graph-database-like answers without pulling the full compile payload. `builder_context` keeps its compatibility operation/response name but emits the current Workshop focus URL, persisted bounded neighborhood, `canvasPosition`, `expected_mtime`, and safe low-level write handoff while declaring that unsaved UI drafts are not included. Repeated read calls inside one MCP server session reuse the compiled artifact while the vault document signature is unchanged, so first-contact agent run orders do not pay the full compile cost for every graph query. `match_nodes` returns a `followUp` packet for the first returned row with ready-to-run `node_profile`, incoming/outgoing `match_edges`, and `blast_radius` MCP calls plus CLI fallback commands, so a graph scan can become focused evidence without another round of tool-selection guesswork. `match_edges` returns a `followUp` packet for the first returned real edge with ready-to-run `explain_relation`, `path`, and `relation_check` MCP calls plus CLI fallback commands, so edge scans move directly into evidence and write-preflight instead of being treated as raw proof. `match_edges.filters`, `match_edges.edges[].relationType`, `followUp.focusEdge.relationType`, and `query_plan(match_edges).normalized` expose public names such as `depends_on` next to canonical frontmatter `types` or `via` values such as `dependencies`, so terminal and MCP clients can show the relation name users typed while keeping executable graph keys. `node_profile.edges.incoming/outgoing.byRelationType` and edge `relationType` expose public names such as `depends_on` for node detail views; `domain_matrix.filters.relationTypes`, `connections.rows[].byRelationType`, and connection examples do the same for coupling views, while canonical `types`, `via`, and `byRelation` stay available for graph-key callers. The UI semantic coupling matrix and CLI node deep dive can be rerun from Claude Code, Codex, or terminal fallbacks with the same user-facing names. `agent_brief` returns Claude Code/Codex handoff readiness, a copyable `handoffPrompt` (also printable via `ontology-atlas agent-brief --prompt`), graph entrypoints, first MCP calls, structured `graphDbQueryPack` (`facets` / `schema` / `query_plan(match_nodes)` / `match_nodes` / `query_plan(match_edges)` / `match_edges` / `domain_matrix` / `query_plan(centrality)` / `centrality` / `query_plan(all_paths)` / `all_paths` / `explain_relation` / `business_questions` outcome, domain-boundary, capability-claim, and implementation-evidence scans), investigation playbooks including `graph_traversal` (`schema` → `query_plan(all_paths)` → `all_paths` → `pattern_walk` / `project_map`), `traversalStrategy` (`plan_before_enumeration` → `bounded_path_evidence` → `containment_cross_check`) for plan-first bounded traversal, per-playbook `evidence[]` and `stopWhen[]` checklists, write guardrails for `add_relation` / rename-merge / post-change sync, relation preflight before `add_relation`, a `relationDecisionGuide` for the `skip_existing` / `review_inverse` / `safe_to_add` / `review_new_schema` outcomes, `resultContracts` requiring `all_paths` callers to report completeness fields and requiring `match_nodes` / `match_edges` callers to report `totalMatches`, `limited`, and `followUp` details before treating scan rows as evidence, and read-first write policy. The CLI companion `ontology-atlas agent-brief [vault] --graph-db-pack` turns that pack into a shell-pasteable graph scan script for sessions without MCP. `relation_check` validates relation `type` before endpoint slug resolution, so relation typos such as `depend_on` still return nearest-value hints even in empty or project-less vaults, and returns `matchingEdges`, reverse-direction `inverseEdges`, and a recommendation decision (`skip_existing`, `review_inverse`, `safe_to_add`, or `review_new_schema`). Non-dependency relations may expose an `add_relation` `proposedAction`; a new `depends_on` returns no executable args and instead exposes `approvalGate.writeAllowed:false` until observable ability, rationale, explicit human approval, and nonblank `why` are present. `maintenance_plan` actions include stable `id`, cursor resume via `afterActionId`, explicit `cursor.reason` metadata, executable graph-array canonicalization, count-safe summary fields, `byPhase` / `bySeverity` / `byKind` remaining-queue buckets, `executable`, current-page `nextExecutableAction`, current-page `nextReviewAction`, plus `executableOnly` / `phases` / `severities` / `kinds` filters; ready pages report `cursor.found=true` with `cursor.reason=null`, while unknown cursors return an empty page with `cursor.found=false`, zero remaining actions, and no next actions. `phases`, `severities`, and `kinds` are enum-validated so typoed work-queue filters fail instead of returning an empty plan.\n`impact` and `blast_radius` follow only the `depends_on` relations that humans have explicitly written. Structural relationships such as what contains what are excluded from impact scope and risk calculations — for such structural questions, `reachability` and `subgraph` provide the answers. Each dependency edge is marked as either `review_required` (needs human review) or `declared_with_rationale` (reason provided), depending on whether a rationale is recorded. Until there is a current-source receipt confirming that each relationship remains factually accurate, the completeness and `risk` of this answer remain `unknown`.\n\n16. **validate_vault** — whole-vault health check with per-file issues and grouped summary, including required/valid/unique UID claims, merge identity history, graph-array canonicality, and dangling graph references\n\n`analyze_repo_structure`'s semantic discovery scans only up to 200 Markdown files and 1,000 directory entries across all three roots. General semantic documents stop reading at 256 KiB before being read. Visited real directories, archives, broken symlinks, or symlinks outside the repository do not expand the scan.\n\nDirect workspace members of `apps/*` and `packages/*` also become candidates for the same 6-document packet, including their static name+description `package.json` and package `README.md`. Only up to 48 members per conventional root are scanned; scripts/dependencies are not read, nor are package names automatically promoted to business meaning.\n\nBusiness capability candidates follow the same principle. They are proposed only when bounded outcome prose and implementation evidence are both confirmed; implementation-oriented folder names like UI, transport, policy, or telemetry are not automatically promoted to business meaning. Without evidence, they remain implementation review targets, and the analyzer does not write to the vault.\n\n17. **analyze_repo_structure** `{ rootPath?, maxDepth?, ignore?, proposal?, qualification? }` — side-effect-free bootstrap candidates from package / README / source layout plus the executable construction lifecycle. A valid complete proposal first returns an exact non-writing `reviewPlan`, plan/source digests, eight phase states, every `requiredGapId`, and a shadow-only `admission` receipt (`self_qualified`, `partial_visible_gap`, `human_review_required`, or `hard_block`); `self_qualified` is an auto-write candidate signal, not write permission. `canWrite` remains false and `writePlan` is absent until the existing human acceptance gate is satisfied. A separately identified evaluator then measures approved executive/employee/FDE/agent CQs, current claims/citations, seven quality axes, the complete source-hidden task, and cold-start/prior-CQ regression. After the user sees the exact plan and accepts its digest/revision plus every visible gap, the unchanged proposal and `constructionQualification:v1` packet may release a `writePlan` exactly equal to the reviewed rows. Maker-only evaluation, missing authority, `not_measured`, stale/private provenance, red mandatory axes, source/plan drift, regression failure, or an unaccepted gap fails closed. Acceptance is declared provenance, not authenticated identity or a truth certificate. Its five proposal competency answers still carry `answered` / `partial` / `visible-gap` plus typed concept, relation, evidence, and path witnesses, and the project body preserves that audit. `Excludes` is reserved for sourced product/concept boundaries: unknown or unmeasured evidence belongs in `Uncertainty` or a competency gap, and `epistemic-exclusion-boundary` blocks a proposal that would persist those unknowns as scope. Root `ARCHITECTURE.md` and classified Markdown under bounded `docs`, `site`, and `website` discovery can join the existing six-document semantic packet; archive-like paths and repository-escaping symlinks cannot. README extraction preserves purpose, responsibility/architecture, and ability blocks inside the existing 1,200-character budget instead of letting sponsor/backer/TOC sections consume it. Root package contracts remain bounded evidence, not meaning nodes: Rust reads allowlisted `Cargo.toml` package/features fields and returns separate literal `cfg`/`cfg_attr` provenance without evaluating predicates, executing code, or allowing relation writes. Python reads bounded static package evidence and import-participating boundaries; unused or unsafe inputs are skipped. Root Go modules contribute at most 24 import-participating package-directory element candidates, never path-derived capabilities. A proposal call recomputes the existing read-only import receipt so selectively proposed TS/JS/Python file endpoints and Go file/package endpoints are validated without relying on prior-call state, and import-backed `depends_on` must match observed direction. After the exact released rows land, the agent validates, compiles, connects the source, and finalizes project meaning.\n18. **infer_imports** `{ rootPath?, sourceFolders?, ignore?, maxFiles?, reviewMode?, afterReviewId? }` — side-effect-free TS/JS plus root-package Python file imports and root-module Go package imports. Existing file/module edges remain unchanged. Go is exposed separately as `packageImportEvidence` contract `goPackageImports:v1`: each row preserves the importing file, repository-relative source and target package directories, literal import spec, production/test role, and value usage without inventing a target file. It reads root-contained module-local imports only, never runs `go`, a compiler, module cache, or network, skips nested modules plus Go build-excluded `vendor`/`testdata`/underscore-prefixed fixture trees, ignores import-shaped lines inside multiline raw strings, caps files with the shared default 5000, caps each file at 256 KiB and 256 imports, and names external Go modules as out of scope. Its `coverage` receipt says which languages are supported; Cargo detection still marks Rust `use`/`mod`/macro dependency graphs unsupported. File and package receipts distinguish source role and usage; `value` does not claim runtime execution. Every collapsed edge includes whole-edge counts, their joint `productValueCount`, and up to five exact evidence receipts. Missing vault edges and Go package evidence are review-only, never executable write proposals. Compact and focus delivery surface Go counts plus the explicit full-evidence call instead of silently dropping a large package graph. CLI `infer-imports --apply` is disabled, and bootstrap/index cannot auto-create import endpoints or semantic `depends_on`; an agent must inspect both concepts, explain the meaning-level dependency, obtain human approval, and supply nonblank `why` before one explicit write.\n19. **index_project** `{ rootPath?, maxFiles?, threshold?, skipImports? }` — side-effect-free project indexing checkpoint that combines repo structure analysis, file-import and Go package-import indexing, and vault validation. It reuses one full import receipt for analyzer evidence, reports file and package relation counts separately, and preserves coverage instead of reducing uncertainty to one count. `plan.conceptDelta` separates raw candidates into existing, ambiguous-alias review, and genuinely new buckets, and `next.reviewCalls` gives exact calls for retrieving full rows before applying anything.\n\nFor `agent_brief`, structural readiness is not meaning confidence. A fresh call\nfor an explicit project derives `meaningAssessment:v1` from three independent\ndimensions: the current graph structure, the versioned competency receipt and\nits typed witness inventory, and project-source provenance/currentness. The\noverall result is categorical (`verified_current`, `needs_evidence`,\n`review_required`, or `invalid`); Atlas emits no combined score or percentage\nthat could hide a stale source or unresolved witness.\n\n`query_ontology({operation:\"cycles\"})` returns each cycle as the canonical slug\npath plus aligned `nodeSummaries[]`, so dependency-cycle diagnostics are readable\nwithout extra node lookups.\n\n#### Write tools (14)\n\nAll destructive dry-runs (`git_snapshot`, relation remove/replace,\nrename/reclassify/merge/delete, and absorb) expose the same agent decision\ncontract: `previewReady`, `canConfirm`, `wouldChange`, and\n`blockedReasons[]`. Tool-specific legacy `ok` is not the confirmation signal.\n\n1. **add_concept** `{ slug, kind, title, domain?, capabilities?, elements?, body? }` — create new `.md`; graph arrays are trimmed, deduped, and sorted on write (throws on existing slug); changed writes return compact `postWriteMaintenance` with `byPhase` / `bySeverity` / `byKind` queue buckets, action `score`, executable `proposedAction`, and current-page next action pointers\n   - **R6 validation**: title must be non-empty trimmed string (`isValidVaultTitle`)\n2. **add_concepts** `{ concepts }` — batch create nodes (max 50), order-preserving partial results; non-object row shape / unknown row field errors are isolated as `{ok:false, error}` rows, single unknown-field rows include `receivedField` plus one-row `unknownFields`, multi unknown-field rows report every offending field with nearest hints, and duplicate input slugs report both the failing `concepts[n]` row and first-seen `concepts[m]` via text plus structured `rowName` / `firstSeenAt`; changed batches return compact `postWriteMaintenance` with `byPhase` / `bySeverity` / `byKind` queue buckets, action `score`, executable `proposedAction`, and current-page next action pointers for the final graph\n3. **patch_concept** `{ slug, frontmatter?, body?, expected_mtime? }` — update existing; graph arrays are canonicalized, but immutable `uid` and merge-owned `merged_uids` cannot be changed by generic patch\n    - **R6 validation**: rejects `title: null` and `title: \"\"`\n    - **R11 conflict guard**: optional `expected_mtime` (from get_concept response). Throws `VaultConflictError` if file mtime differs at write time — caller re-reads and retries.\n4. **add_relation** `{ from, to, type }` — append to source frontmatter graph key; invalid relation `type` is rejected before endpoint slug resolution with a closest-value hint plus structured `valueName` / `receivedValue` / `suggestion` / `allowedValues`; changed writes return compact `postWriteMaintenance` with `byPhase` / `bySeverity` / `byKind` queue buckets, action `score`, executable `proposedAction`, and current-page next action pointers\n    - type enum: `depends_on` (→ `dependencies`) / `relates` / `contains` / `describes` / `domains` / `capabilities` / `elements` / `domain`\n    - **R7 validation**: both `from` AND `to` slug must exist in vault (`vaultSlugExists`)\n    - Unique tail aliases and frontmatter `slug:` aliases are resolved to canonical file slugs before write\n    - Idempotent: duplicate returns `{ alreadyExists: true }`\n5. **add_relations** `{ relations }` — batch edge writer (max 50), idempotent per row; non-object row shape / unknown row field errors are isolated as `{ok:false, error}` rows, single unknown-field rows include `receivedField` plus one-row `unknownFields`, multi unknown-field rows report every offending field with nearest hints plus structured `rowName` / `allowedFields` / `receivedFields`, and relation type typos include structured `valueName` / `receivedValue` / `suggestion` / `allowedValues`; stored relation arrays are deduped and sorted as canonical graph sets; changed batches return compact `postWriteMaintenance` with `byPhase` / `bySeverity` / `byKind` queue buckets, action `score`, executable `proposedAction`, and current-page next action pointers for the final graph\n6. **delete_concept** `{ slug, confirm?, force?, expected_mtime? }` — permanent delete; confirmed deletes return compact `postWriteMaintenance` with `byPhase` / `bySeverity` / `byKind` queue buckets, action `score`, executable `proposedAction`, and current-page next action pointers\n    - `confirm: false` (dry-run with backlinks preview) / `true` (actual)\n    - `force: false` (throw if backlinks exist) / `true` (delete anyway)\n    - **R11 conflict guard**: optional `expected_mtime`\n7. **rename_concept** `{ oldSlug, newSlug, confirm?, overwrite? }` — **R11** atomic graph-level rename\n    - Moves the .md file, updates the moved file's `slug:` key, rewrites every backlink (frontmatter array entries, inline string keys like `domain`, body links `[[oldSlug]]` / `(oldSlug.md)`)\n    - Tail-only references (`mcp-server` for `capabilities/mcp-server`) also redirected to the new tail\n    - `confirm: false` (dry-run with full update preview) / `true` (actual)\n    - Confirmed renames return compact `postWriteMaintenance` with `byPhase` / `bySeverity` / `byKind` queue buckets, action `score`, executable `proposedAction`, and current-page next action pointers\n    - Replaces the manual `find_backlinks` + N `patch_concept` loop\n    - Preserves the node UID; only the readable address and backlinks change\n8. **merge_concepts** `{ fromSlug, intoSlug, confirm?, expected_mtime?, expected_into_mtime? }` — **R11** atomic graph-level merge\n    - Redirects every backlink `fromSlug` → `intoSlug`, then deletes `fromSlug.md`\n    - Preserves `intoSlug` UID and records source identity history in canonical `merged_uids`; prose is not auto-combined\n    - For confirmed writes, pass both source and survivor mtimes so neither concurrent edit is overwritten\n    - `confirm: false` (dry-run) / `true` (actual)\n    - Confirmed merges return compact `postWriteMaintenance` with `byPhase` / `bySeverity` / `byKind` queue buckets, action `score`, executable `proposedAction`, and current-page next action pointers\n9. **remove_relation** `{ from, to, type, confirm?, expected_mtime? }` — one exact typed relation and its rationale, dry-run first; an absent relation is an explicit no-op blocker\n10. **replace_relation** `{ from, oldTo, oldType, newTo, newType, why?, confirm?, expected_mtime? }` — relation target/type/rationale replacement in one source-file write\n11. **reclassify_concept** `{ slug, newKind, newSlug?, domain?, body?, confirm?, expected_mtime? }` — kind/slug/domain transition with backlink redirect and generated-starter handling\n12. **absorb_document** `{ filePath, confirm?, allowOutsideRepo? }` — classifies a local agent-instruction document, writes accepted policy nodes, backs up the source, then rewrites it as a slim pointer. Canonical paths outside `repoRoot`, including symlink escapes, are blocked until a reviewed dry-run is repeated with `allowOutsideRepo:true`\n13. **git_snapshot** `{ confirm?, expectedHead?, message? }` — validates and commits only the active vault pathspec locally; blocks stale HEAD, detached HEAD, Git operations in progress, and validator errors; never pushes\n14. **finalize_project_meaning** `{ projectSlug, expected_mtime }` — post-write finalization for one explicit project. It reads the five competency answers from the current project Markdown; after current vault validation and a complete project scope, it resolves every typed witness against the current graph/source inventory and stores a versioned receipt with optimistic-concurrency protection. It stores no raw answers or private source coordinates. `ok: true` means the receipt write succeeded, not that the ontology or source is verified; callers read the returned categorical `meaningAssessment` or a fresh explicit-project `agent_brief`.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Cross-cutting UI\n\n**feat/rail-rollout** collapsed the old 3-tier nav (`OperationsNav` top tabs +\n`OntologySubNav` inline sub-tabs + `BottomTabBar`) into one ownership model: a\npersistent left rail on desktop and `BottomTabBar` on mobile. They share the\nsame active-destination resolver while exposing inventories appropriate to\ntheir viewport. `OperationsNav` and `OntologySubNav` are retired (deleted, not\njust unmounted).\n\n### `AppNavRail` (desktop, `lg:` and up — left side, on every page)\n- 6 destinations: Map (`/`, `/topology`) · Docs (`/docs`) · Insights\n  (`/ontology/insights`) · Projects (`/projects` or `/project/*`) · Agents\n  (`/agents`) · Git (`/git`). Workshop is the map's contextual relation-writing\n  surface, not a rail destination.\n- Bottom utility tier: the `settingsSlot` plus the web-only Get App tile.\n  `AppShell` supplies the app-wide settings trigger by default; a page can\n  override the slot for a surface-specific control.\n- Active-item detection: shared `resolveActiveNavDestination`\n  (`src/shared/lib/nav-destination.ts`) — `BottomTabBar` uses the same semantic\n  resolver, so a route has one destination even when mobile intentionally\n  omits its button.\n\n### `AppSettingsMenu` (app shell + contextual page headers)\n- The old 5-tab settings modal is now one compact settings sheet\n  (`src/widgets/app-settings-menu`): screen controls, workspace, and the AI\n  agent entry are scanned in one column. `LocaleSwitch` is an immediate screen\n  control; the long MCP connection proof stays behind the AI agent drill-in.\n- **AI Connection** (`AiConnectionPanel`, 2026-07-26) — a second drill-in row for\n  your own API key: store it in the operating-system credential store (desktop only), check the\n  connection with a request that carries **0 vault characters**, and read the\n  tail of `.ontology-atlas/llm-audit.jsonl` where every call is recorded. The\n  key is written once and never readable back (only its last 4 characters);\n  the Rust side refuses to send at all when the audit line cannot be appended\n  (log-before-send). Audit writes reject symbolic/hard-linked and non-regular files, hold one\n  exclusive reservation per vault, and recheck the reserved tail before\n  finalizing; existing audit files are narrowed to owner-only `0600`. This\n  native LLM path is currently enabled only on Unix/macOS;\n  the public Windows beta fails closed until equivalent reparse-point and\n  file-identity proof exists (the map, vault, and bundled MCP remain available).\n  In the browser the key field is not rendered — the card\n  explains why storage is desktop-only and links to `/download`. There is no\n  chat surface: the panel says in plain words that asking your vault is still\n  being shaped.\n  - **Named vendors: Anthropic · OpenAI · Google Gemini — frozen at three.**\n    All three share one concept (paste a key → OS credential store → last 4 → check), so\n    the third costs the reader nothing new. A fourth is admitted only when it\n    both (a) uses an auth protocol that a Bearer-compatible arm cannot absorb\n    and (b) has demand evidence; every other vendor is meant for the\n    user-typed-address arm, which ships together with the feature that\n    consumes it. Gemini authenticates through the `x-goog-api-key` header —\n    never the documented `?key=` query form, because a URL is a place that\n    gets logged.\n  - **Connect by Address — Models Running on My Computer (2026-08-01).** Below\n    the three named vendors, the fourth row is not a specific vendor but a single\n    **entry point** that accepts any runner (runner = the program actually executing the model). Enter the runner address\n    (default `http://localhost:11434`) and press [Check Connection]; this single request\n    answers three things together: \"Is it alive? · Does it speak in OpenAI-compatible format? · Which models can be selected?\". Installed models arrive as a list, so the user **only needs to choose** (no typos from typing names). No API key is needed — this branch bypasses the key vault entirely. Ollama · LM Studio · llama.cpp server · vLLM all enter through this single entry point (addresses use OpenAI-compatible `/v1/*`. If each runner required a unique custom API, separate conversion code would have been needed for each runner).\n    - **Different messages are shown for each failure reason** — distinguishing cases where the runner is down (connection itself fails), another program is running on that port (404), or no models are installed, and noting what to do next for each.\n    - **Unencrypted `http` is allowed only within this machine (loopback).**\n      To point to an external machine, `https` is required, and addresses containing username/password are rejected — because the address remains in logs as-is.\n    - **\"Does not go out\" is stated only when true.** If the address points to this computer, it writes \"It does not leave this computer, and the log records the destination as `localhost:11434` — that is proof it did not leave.\" If the user points to another machine via `https`, this sentence is replaced with \"This address is outside this computer.\"\n    - This branch cannot be used in web browsers (browser pages cannot send requests to the user's computer localhost). Therefore, a card stating \"Not available here\" explains the reason **separately** from the API key vault story and links to `/download`.\n  - Every recorded call names its destination host. The audit line carries\n    `host` (e.g. `generativelanguage.googleapis.com`), and the screen states\n    that host before you press check — the strongest claim we can prove for a\n    named vendor is \"it only goes to the official address compiled into the\n    code\". `host` was added without bumping the schema `v`, so lines written\n    before it exist read back fine with a `null` destination.\n  - Unregistered vendors collapse to a one-line `name · [Add key]` row that\n    expands in place, one at a time — three always-open password fields would\n    turn a settings sheet into a form gate.\n- **Runtime** (`AcpRuntimeSettings`, 2026-08-16, desktop app only) — A section where the app finds and displays coding agents (Claude Code, Codex, etc.) already installed on this computer. The one thing this section does is **tell you what can be used right now**.\n  - The list splits into two branches: \"Ready to use\" is expanded, while \"Requires installation\" is collapsed. Reasons for not being usable are split into four categories: requires installation / needs Node / needs uv / manual installation. Since the user's task differs per category, they are not lumped together as just \"installed/not installed.\" Press [Re-check] to scan again at any time.\n  - **The list comes from an ACP registry snapshot committed at build time**\n    (`src-tauri/src/acp-registry.json`, `scripts/build-acp-registry.mjs`,\n    updates via `pnpm acp:registry`). It does not call a CDN at runtime, so the list remains available offline, and changes are recorded in git diff. Icons are also fetched at build time and bundled in `public/acp-icons/` for the same reason (since the registry spec uses 16×16 monochrome SVGs, brand colors do not enter the app).\n  - **In-app chat requires an app-owned permission gate.** Claude Agent qualifies through an isolated `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` and linked existing credential. Codex remains detected and keeps its external MCP setup path, but is not offered for in-app chat: its `read-only` mode blocks direct file writes without stopping an Atlas MCP mutation loaded from the vault's own Codex config.\n  - **Atlas MCP and provider traffic are separate boundaries.** The Atlas MCP server is a local stdio child with no daemon, port, or network request. The coding agent using it may send prompts, context, and tool results to its own provider.\n  - Modes measured to remove the permission gate are hidden. Unmeasured modes remain explicitly unverified; they are never treated as safe by default.\n  - Processes cannot be launched in browsers. On the web, a single line explaining why it doesn't work and where it does replaces the list.\n- The persistent shell mounts the rail settings trigger. Current agent work is\n  exposed by `AgentActivityChip` in Topology's contextual map controls, while\n  `AgentActivitySettings` controls its visibility and notifications in the\n  settings sheet; neither is a navigation destination.\n\n### `BottomTabBar` (mobile only, `lg:` hidden)\n- 4 core destinations: Map · Docs · Insights · Projects. Contextual relation\n  writing and Git remain desktop workbench actions, and the retired ERD builder\n  tab was removed 2026-07-24. Web adds Get App as a fifth utility, not a\n  destination.\n- Min height 56 px (safe-area)\n- Hidden only on the standalone `/download/` surface. Root without a loaded\n  vault is the gateway; after a vault loads, root shares the map destination.\n\n### Search palettes (separate by design — R5 skip merge)\n- **`⌘K` `SearchPalette`** — projects-focused fuzzy search + top vault docs match (3) + recent (5) + Layer filter (All / Hub / Node)\n- **`⇧⌘K` `MountedGlobalSearch`** — ontology nodes + projects unified (`cmdk`-based, kind/project filter chips, virtualized)\n- Both palettes share keyboard: `↑↓` navigate · `↵` select · `Esc` close\n\n### `ShortcutSheet` (`?` to open)\n- 10 sections grouped: navigation · topology · search palette · hub rail · workspace palette · workspace graph · workspace files · workspace actions · tour · portfolio\n- 2-column grid on sm+, focus trap, `Esc` closes\n\n### `LocaleSwitch`\n- Two-button toggle EN / KO\n- Replaces only the locale prefix while preserving the raw query and hash,\n  including duplicate-key order and existing encoding. Uses\n  `router.replace(..., {scroll: false})`, so changing the language does not add\n  browser history or reset URL-addressed task state such as the selected\n  Insights tab.\n- localStorage `ontology-atlas:locale`\n\n### `ThemeToggle`\n- Moon / Sun icon toggle\n- SSR-safe (mount-state placeholder until first useEffect)\n- `html[data-theme]` attribute\n\n---\n\n## 5. docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-D1-off-r1.txt:696:docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-B1-on.txt:10112:src/entities/docs-vault/data/content.json-16-  "FOUNDATIONS": "# FOUNDATIONS — what grounds ontology-atlas\n\n> This document ties the product to **public, citable academic/standard/craft references** rather than *feelings*.\n> A collection of grounds to prevent \"building without even knowing what ontology is\" — what already exists, and where/how we make *our own thing* differently on top of it.\n>\n> **Citation discipline:** All references cited here are public papers / W3C standards / public books / public blogs.\n> The initial set was re-verified with original text and official metadata on 2026-06-01, and the ontology-construction reinforcement set on 2026-08-09. Links/citations are provided, but the body text is not copied. When adding new references, maintain public, citable, and directly verified standards, and do not cite materials read only via search results.\n\nThis is a living document. When we make a design or feature decision, we should be able to point\nat *which* of these it descends from — or argue explicitly why we diverge.\n\n---\n\n## 1. What an ontology actually is\n\nThe word is older than software (Aristotle's *categories of being*), but our usage is the\n**information-science / knowledge-representation** one.\n\n- **Gruber (1993)** gave the most-cited definition: *\"an ontology is an explicit specification of a\n  conceptualization\"* — a representational vocabulary (classes, relations, functions, objects) for a\n  shared domain, designed so knowledge can be **shared and reused** across AI systems.\n  → Our vault's markdown frontmatter *is* \"an explicit specification of a conceptualization\" of a\n  codebase. The exact Atlas application model — five authorable kinds, one reserved reader kind,\n  relation support layers, `is_a` test, and inference boundary — lives only in\n  [`ONTOLOGY-ATLAS-SPEC.md` §2](ONTOLOGY-ATLAS-SPEC.md#2-the-five-authorable-node-kinds-and-reserved-reader-kind).\n  The name is earned by that shared meaning model, not by pretending the files implement a standard\n  they do not.\n- **Studer, Benjamins & Fensel (1998)** sharpened it to the canonical four-part definition:\n  *\"a **formal, explicit specification** of a **shared conceptualization**.\"* Word by word:\n  *conceptualization* = an abstract model of phenomena; *explicit* = concept types and constraints\n  are defined, not implied; *formal* requires a representation with defined semantics, not merely text\n  that a parser can read; *shared* = held in common rather than private.\n  → Atlas is explicit, shared, and machine-parseable. Its current Markdown schema does **not** claim\n  model-theoretic semantics, logical completeness, or a DL reasoner, so “machine-readable = formal” is\n  not our claim. This distinction is a trust boundary, not a downgrade.\n- **Noy & McGuinness, \"Ontology Development 101\" (Stanford, 2001)** is the practical methodology:\n  define classes + a hierarchy, define properties (slots) + constraints (facets), then instances —\n  and crucially, *\"ontology development is iterative, there is no single correct ontology, and the\n  right design depends on the intended application and anticipated extensions.\"*\n  → This is our **grow-the-vault-each-session, agent-maintained, no-one-true-model** stance, verbatim.\n\n### The formality spectrum — and where we deliberately sit\n\nThe W3C Semantic Web stack is the standard reference frame:\n\n- **RDF 1.1 (2014 Recommendation)** — RDF graphs are sets of subject-predicate-object triples whose\n  terms are IRIs, blank nodes, or datatyped literals. RDF 1.2 reached Candidate Recommendation Snapshot\n  on 2026-04-07; that work-in-progress status is not an endorsement or an Atlas compatibility claim.\n  An Atlas edge can be *mapped* to a triple-like statement, but the vault itself is not an RDF\n  serialization and does not inherit RDF identity or entailment semantics.\n- **OWL 2 (2012)** — the **heavyweight** end: classes/properties/individuals with **description-logic\n  reasoning** (consistency checking, classification, inference) under an open-world assumption. Atlas\n  does not adopt OWL vocabulary, class/individual semantics, or inference. Missing Atlas evidence is\n  `unknown`/a visible gap according to our application contract, not an OWL entailment result.\n- **SKOS (2009)** — the **lightweight** end: `skos:Concept` + `broader`/`narrower`/`related`.\n  `skos:broader` names a direct broader link and is not itself transitive; Atlas borrows that caution,\n  not SKOS semantics or conformance.\n- **SHACL 1.0 (2017 Recommendation)** — validates an RDF data graph against a separate shapes graph\n  and returns a validation report; it does not make OWL a closed-world schema language. SHACL 1.2 Core\n  was still a Working Draft on 2026-08-03. Atlas's validator plays an analogous product role over\n  Markdown, but it is an Atlas schema validator, not a SHACL processor.\n\n> **Our position on the spectrum:** a lightweight, application-specific ontology and executable\n> meaning model — more structured than tags, deliberately less expressive than RDF/OWL. Human-and-agent\n> readable Markdown, typed application relations, deterministic validation/query, no reasoner, no\n> backend. “Graph-shaped” describes the implementation; it does not assert standards conformance.\n\n### Construction is a requirements-and-tests lifecycle, not noun extraction\n\nThe literature converges on one discipline Atlas adopts: **start from the decisions the ontology must\nsupport, not from a directory tree or a list of nouns.** Grüninger & Fox make competency questions\nrequirements and benchmarks; NeOn and LOT put requirements, reuse, implementation, publication, and\nmaintenance in one lifecycle; SAMOD turns a motivating scenario, CQs, a glossary, exemplar data, and\nqueries into a regression-carrying test case.\n\nAtlas translates that lineage into an eight-stage, local-first construction contract:\n\n1. **Purpose and authority** — name the product outcome, intended users/decisions, scope, non-goals,\n   domain-language owners, and sources before proposing concepts.\n2. **Requirements as competency questions** — derive CQs from motivating scenarios; give each an\n   audience, expected answer shape, required witness kinds, explicit unknown/refusal behavior, and\n   owner approval. An LLM may draft a CQ, but may not approve its own requirement.\n3. **Evidence inventory and reuse** — separate direct, triangulated, structural, conflicting, and\n   absent evidence; search the existing vault before minting a term. A folder name is not a domain and\n   an import edge is not automatically a business dependency.\n4. **Small conceptual slice** — propose the smallest project→domain→capability→element slice that\n   answers a coherent CQ set. Give every concept an intensional definition, includes/excludes, examples,\n   counterexamples, and uncertainty. Do not expand the schema to fill a template.\n5. **Semantic and structural tests** — reject circular definitions, duplicate siblings, category\n   mistakes, dangling references, predicate-direction mistakes, and unsupported `is_a`. Same domain,\n   naming similarity, or folder nesting alone never proves subsumption.\n6. **Functional and pragmatic tests** — replay CQs against exemplar facts and expected answers, then\n   test whether a source-hidden person/agent can make the intended decision with bounded calls and\n   supported claims. A valid file is not necessarily a useful ontology.\n7. **Human acceptance and provenance** — persist only the accepted write plan; keep source spans,\n   graph/source digests, rejected/partial CQs, and the responsible human decision visible. Receipts are\n   provenance, not truth certificates.\n8. **Regression and evolution** — after each accepted change, rerun prior CQs, graph/schema validation,\n   source currentness, impact, and handoff checks. Git records change; the ontology still has to explain\n   why the meaning changed and which requirement authorized it.\n\nRecent LLM evidence makes the human-sovereign boundary stronger, not weaker. LLM-generated ontology\ndrafts can outperform novices in bounded studies, but quality remains variable; one 2025 CQ pipeline\nreported only about a quarter of outputs passing scope/relevance and fewer than half as unproblematic.\nThe 2026 IDEA2 workflow therefore separates LLM elicitation from domain-expert validation and records\nthe full CQ revision provenance. Atlas follows that separation: **agents accelerate elicitation,\nconstruction, and repair; people own meaning and accepted requirements; an independent evaluator owns\nqualification.**\n\n### Quality is a vector; no green total may hide a red dimension\n\nOQuaRE treats an ontology both as a software artifact and as a tool that must be useful. Atlas keeps\nthat separation and does not reduce construction quality to node count, fan-out, compiler health, or a\nsingle confidence score.\n\n| Dimension | Atlas question | Required evidence | Fail-closed result |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Semantic correctness | Are kind, boundary, and predicate meanings defensible? | definitions, includes/excludes, examples/counterexamples, relation rationale | reject or `review_required` |\n| Structural conformance | Does the Markdown graph satisfy its declared schema? | parser/compiler/validator results, resolved references | block invalid writes/finalization |\n| Functional adequacy | Can it answer the approved CQs? | expected answer + quantified witness coverage | `partial` / `visible-gap` |\n| Evidence and provenance | Can each business claim be traced to current sources and an authority? | citations, source spans/digests, approval and receipt lineage | unsupported / stale |\n| Pragmatic usefulness | Can each audience make the intended decision without hidden source access? | source-hidden task result, claim ledger, time/calls | unknown / not-qualified |\n| Maintainability | Can a change be reviewed and prior meaning retested? | git diff, impact path, CQ regression, source-currentness check | maintenance action required |\n| Interoperability honesty | Are exports and standards claims limited to what is implemented? | declared format/profile and round-trip contract | no RDF/OWL/SHACL conformance claim |\n\nStructural metrics and automated judges are diagnostic signals, not acceptance authorities. OOPS! and\nOntoClean can expose modeling pitfalls; LLM graph judges can help triage noisy extraction; none replaces\ndomain requirements, exact witnesses, counterexamples, or independent task-level evaluation.\n\nThe re-executable evaluator packet, categorical verdict, privacy boundary, and representative fixture\nare specified in [Ontology Construction Qualification v1](ONTOLOGY-CONSTRUCTION-QUALIFICATION.md).\n\nFor Atlas, the conservative direct-subsumption test is executable prose, not a reasoner claim: both\nendpoints have the same `domain`/`capability`/`element` kind, every valid narrower example satisfies the\nbroader definition, the distinction is not synonymy or membership/part/dependency/sequence, and no known\naccepted concept fits between them. The normative rule and current `broader` write boundary live in the\nsame [meta-model contract](ONTOLOGY-ATLAS-SPEC.md#22-direct-is_a--broader-test).\n\n---\n\n## 2. Agent memory & LLM × knowledge-graph (the live field, 2023–2026)\n\nOur wedge — *\"the AI agent forgets the codebase every session; this is git-native ontology memory it\nmaintains and queries\"* — sits inside an active, recognized research lineage. We are **not** inventing\nthe category; we are taking a specific, opinionated position in it.\n\n**The \"LLM forgets; give it external memory\" premise:**\n\n- **MemGPT (Packer et al., 2023)** — \"virtual context management\": the model pages information between\n  a fixed context window (fast memory) and external stores (slow memory) via self-issued function\n  calls, simulating unbounded context. → We are the **codebase-specific, structured & human-readable\n  version of MemGPT's \"slow memory\"**: the agent pages the repo's mental model in/out through a runtime-advertised MCP surface\n  instead of opaque archival storage.\n- **\"A Survey on the Memory Mechanism of LLM-based Agents\" (Zhang et al., ACM TOIS, 2024)** — the\n  peer-reviewed taxonomy of memory *sources / forms / operations* and *evaluation*. → Lets us name our\n  choices precisely: **structured (KG) over textual** memory, **write-on-task-completion** operations,\n  **human-readable + git-versioned** storage, **codebase** domain. Safest single category-overview citation.\n\n**Memory as a maintained knowledge graph (the closest analogues):**\n\n- **Zep / Graphiti (Rasmussen et al., 2025)** — agent memory as a **temporal knowledge graph** with a\n  bi-temporal model (event time + ingestion time). The single closest published analogue to us: both\n  treat long-term agent memory as an explicit, maintained graph of typed entities + relations, not a\n  vector blob. → **Where we differ:** we get temporality *for free* from **git history + markdown\n  diffs** rather than a dual-timeline DB; our domain is the **codebase**, not conversation; our store\n  is **plain files in the repo**, not a graph DB.\n- **Mem0 (Chhikara et al., ECAI 2025)** — production memory with a plain-extraction mode *and* a\n  graph mode (`Mem0g`); emphasizes token-cost / latency savings from retrieving a small relevant slice\n  instead of full context. → Mirrors our choice to make **the graph the primary artifact** (markdown\n  frontmatter *is* the graph), and supplies our pragmatic argument: a maintained ontology lets the\n  agent fetch a small relevant subgraph instead of re-reading the whole repo each session.\n\n**LLM × KG more broadly:**\n\n- **Pan et al., \"Unifying LLMs and Knowledge Graphs: A Roadmap\" (IEEE TKDE, 2024)** — the canonical\n  framing of three patterns: KG-enhanced LLMs, **LLM-augmented KGs** (the LLM does construction /\n  completion / QA), and **synergized** LLM+KG as equals. KGs are positioned as *explicit, interpretable,\n  editable* stores that compensate for the LLM black box. → This *is* our core thesis. Our vault loop is\n  \"LLM-augmented KG + synergized\": the agent both **queries** the graph for grounding and\n  **maintains/extends** it.\n- **GraphRAG (Edge et al., Microsoft, 2024; + `microsoft/graphrag`)** — an LLM derives an entity graph\n  from documents, pre-summarizes communities, and answers *global* questions flat vector-RAG can't.\n  → The flagship example of an LLM **building and querying a graph as a memory/index layer**; we apply\n  the same pattern to a **codebase**. **Where we differ:** GraphRAG produces a generated artifact store;\n  our graph is a **human-editable, diff-reviewable markdown vault** that is the source of truth.\n- **\"LLMs on Graphs: A Comprehensive Survey\" (Jin et al., TKDE, 2023/2024)** — vocabulary for what we\n  are: a **text-attributed graph** (frontmatter nodes + typed edges) an LLM reasons over (as\n  predictor / encoder / aligner). Grounds the claim that LLMs *can* reason over typed-edge graphs, not\n  only prose.\n- **\"LLM-empowered Knowledge Graph Construction: A Survey\" (Bian, 2025, preprint)** — schema-based vs\n  schema-free KG construction, and explicitly names **\"dynamic memory for agentic systems\"** as a\n  direction. → Our per-kind normalized frontmatter schema is the schema-based pole; \"dynamic memory for\n  agentic systems\" is our exact positioning.\n- **\"Towards Agentic RAG with Deep Reasoning: A Survey\" (2025, preprint)** — \"agentic RAG\": an agent\n  loops over an external store to ground multi-step reasoning. → Frames our loop in current terms:\n  RAG-*enhanced reasoning* over a maintained graph, not one-shot retrieval.\n\n---\n\n## 3. The codebase side — code knowledge graphs\n\nThe other half of our lineage: representing **code** as a queryable graph rather than re-parsing it\neach time. The premise that a codebase's meaning is best captured as a graph of typed nodes + typed\nedges is well-established academic and industrial prior art.\n\n- **Code Property Graphs (Yamaguchi et al., IEEE S&P 2014)** — fuse AST + control-flow + program-\n  dependence into one queryable graph; express patterns as graph traversals (found 18 unknown Linux\n  vulnerabilities). → The foundational academic precedent for \"**a codebase's meaning is a unified\n  graph you traverse, not re-derive.**\" We fuse `project/domain/capability/element` + edges into one\n  vault the same way.\n- **Glean (Meta, open-source, 2024)** — schema-defined **typed facts** about code in a queryable fact\n  DB, interrogated with a Datalog-style language; powers navigation across a monorepo. → The closest\n  *industrial* analogue. **Contrast:** Glean = machine-*generated* facts; we = **human + agent-maintained,\n  git-native, markdown-as-source-of-truth.**\n- **SCIP (Sourcegraph, 2022)** — a portable, **human-readable**, language-agnostic code-index protocol\n  with stable string symbol IDs. → Validates our choice of a **portable, human-readable, on-disk**\n  representation (markdown frontmatter; slug-keyed nodes) over an opaque binary index.\n- **CodeQL (GitHub/Semmle)** — \"**treat code as data**\": extract a relational DB of facts, query it to\n  find patterns/variants. → The mainstream embodiment of \"**query your codebase like a database**\" —\n  precisely what we offer an agent via focused MCP graph queries (`find_path`, `find_backlinks`, …)\n  instead of\n  re-reading files.\n- **tree-sitter (Brunsfeld et al.)** — incremental parsing → concrete syntax trees with an\n  S-expression query system; the substrate beneath most code indexers (and our own CodeGraph index).\n  → The **structural** layer (\"what symbols exist, how they nest\"). We layer the **meaning** layer\n  (domains, capabilities, evidence, impact) *on top* — that separation is the point.\n\n> **The gap we fill:** CPG / Glean / CodeQL / tree-sitter answer *structural* questions\n> (what calls what, what's defined where) — machine-derived, exhaustive, regenerated. We hold the\n> **human-and-agent-meaningful** layer: *why* this exists, *which capability* it serves, *what breaks*\n> if it changes — curated (not exhaustive), maintained by the developer + agent together, in git.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Design lineage — restraint as craft, cited\n\nOur \"Linear-restrained\" design language (`docs/DESIGN-SYSTEM.md`, `.claude/rules/design.md`) is an\napplied reading of public, citable design thinking — not arbitrary taste.\n\n- **Dieter Rams, \"Ten Principles for Good Design\" (Vitsœ)** — *unobtrusive, honest, long-lasting,\n  thorough to the last detail,* and *\"as little design as possible\"* (\"Less, but better\").\n  → The rationale for neutral greys + a single indigo and the bans on glow/neon/gradients/glassmorphism.\n  Our whole forbidden-pattern list is applied Rams.\n- **Edward Tufte, *The Visual Display of Quantitative Information*** — **graphical integrity**\n  (representation proportional to the quantities) and **direct labelling** (a legend means the mark\n  cannot explain itself). → Grounds honest, proportional rendering of relations so agent and developer\n  read the *same true* model.\n  ⚠️ **The data-ink ratio is cited here as an aesthetic, not as our judgment rule** — it has been\n  tested and did not survive as one. Inbar, Tractinsky & Meyer (ECCE 2007) had 87 participants rate a\n  standard bar graph against its Tufte-minimalist twin and found a clear preference for the\n  non-minimalist version; Bateman et al. (CHI 2010) found embellished charts were described no less\n  accurately than plain ones and were recalled *significantly better* after two to three weeks.\n  Neither study touches graphical integrity, which is why that half stands. When a seat needs to\n  reject a mark, the rule it must cite is Mackinlay's expressiveness, below — \"this ink is not data\"\n  is an assertion those two papers can be pointed at, while \"this mark encodes no typed fact\" is not.\n- **Jock D. Mackinlay, \"Automating the Design of Graphical Presentations of Relational Information\"\n  (ACM TOG 5(2), 1986)** — **expressiveness** (a graphical language must encode the facts in the set,\n  *and no additional facts*) and **effectiveness** (given several expressive encodings, prefer the one\n  the human visual system reads best, ranking channels after Cleveland & McGill). → **This is the rule\n  the design bench actually runs**, and it is what the Infovis seat's mark → typed-fact table is: a\n  mark that maps to no fact fails expressiveness, and a mark that asserts a fact the data does not\n  contain (a decorative rail implying a category) fails it in the other direction. Effectiveness is\n  why `.claude/rules/design.md` sends bar identity to position/length/order/label rather than hue, and\n  why hue-only separation is a defect rather than a preference.\n- **Wathan & Schoger, *Refactoring UI*** (also the Tailwind authors) — establish hierarchy by\n  **de-emphasizing**, use a constrained spacing/type scale, **limit the palette deliberately**.\n  → The concrete how-to behind our `@theme` token scale, the per-screen gutter/spacing consistency\n  work, and the \"no second coloring system\" rule.\n- **John Maeda, *The Laws of Simplicity*** — *Reduce* and *Organize* (\"make a system of many appear\n  fewer\"); *\"subtract the obvious, add the meaningful.\"* → Justifies the kind hierarchy + typed\n  relations as the organizing device, and the agent's job of maintaining a **lean, high-signal** memory\n  graph rather than dumping everything.\n- **Karri Saarinen / Linear, \"Why is quality so rare?\"** — quality is a deliberate daily choice and a\n  strategic advantage; it spreads by advocacy, not ads; craft = believing it matters + skill to\n  recognize it + care for the user. → The canonical public statement of the philosophy our design\n  descends from — restraint as a *quality* decision that wins against louder AI-generated-UI clichés.\n- **Rauno Freiberg, \"Craft\" (rauno.me)** — exceptional UI is *invisible details* (microinteractions,\n  states, motion, polish) you feel, not flashy moments. → Our bar for hover/focus states, transitions,\n  and the topology's feel — *without* the forbidden flashy patterns.\n- **Emil Kowalski, \"Great animations\" (emilkowal.ski)** — natural physics-based motion; sub-300ms\n  ease-out; **purposeful** (convey state, not decorate); 60fps via transform/opacity only;\n  interruptible; respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. → Concrete, citable rules behind our motion budget\n  (`transition-colors`/opacity, sub-200ms, minimal transform). Justifies how the live topology animates\n  as the agent grows the vault: **state-conveying, not glow/pulse decoration.**\n- **Vercel Geist** & **Radix Primitives** — public reference points for the developer-tool aesthetic\n  (high-contrast accessible color, mono for code/diagrams) and the **\"unstyled accessible primitive +\n  our own theming\"** split we follow (inspector, forms, palette; the recent a11y label-association work).\n\n---\n\n## 5. Our own thing — what's genuinely ours\n\nEverything above already exists. Our contribution is a **specific combination** none of the prior art\nholds all of at once:\n\n1. **Agent-maintained** — the AI coding agent reads *and writes* the graph directly through the MCP server's runtime-advertised inventory,\n   not a human-only ontology editor (Protégé) and not a machine-only fact generator (Glean/CodeQL).\n2. **Git-native, markdown-IS-the-graph** — the frontmatter is the source of truth, reviewable as a\n   diff, versioned by git, no backend / no DB. (Zep/Graphiti = graph DB; GraphRAG = generated store;\n   mem0 = service. We = files in your repo.)\n3. **Live topology as the surface** — the same graph is the *human comprehension surface*: you watch it\n   grow as the agent edits, and you edit it back. (Mackinlay expressiveness + Linear's craft, applied to a KG.)\n4. **Codebase-scoped meaning layer** — not conversation memory (Zep/mem0) and not structural code facts\n   (tree-sitter/Glean), but the *why/owns/impacts* layer **on top of** structure, curated by developer +\n   agent.\n5. **Evidence-bound meaning receipts** — an agent can finalize five competency\n   answers, but the receipt is provenance, not an oracle. Every fresh project\n   brief rechecks the current graph, typed witnesses, and source receipt, and\n   keeps structural readiness, competency coverage, and source currentness as\n   separate categorical dimensions. This follows the same inspectability\n   principle as the vault itself: no combined confidence percentage can hide a\n   stale source or an unresolved witness.\n\n**The honest framing:** *agent memory = a maintained knowledge graph* (Zep, Pan et al., the memory\nsurvey all agree). We take the most **inspectable, lowest-infrastructure** position in that space — an\napplication-specific, graph-shaped meaning model that lives as Markdown in the repo, maintained by the\nagent and judged by the human. It is informed by ontology engineering and Semantic Web standards\nwithout claiming RDF/OWL/SKOS conformance. That is the thing to make excellent.\n\n---\n\n## References (public sources; initial set 2026-06-01, construction set 2026-08-09)\n\n**Ontology theory & standards**\n- Gruber, T. R. docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-D1-off-r1.txt:1874:docs/FEATURES.md:945:`impact` and `blast_radius` follow only the `depends_on` relations that humans have explicitly written. Structural relationships such as what contains what are excluded from impact scope and risk calculations — for such structural questions, `reachability` and `subgraph` provide the answers. Each dependency edge is marked as either `review_required` (needs human review) or `declared_with_rationale` (reason provided), depending on whether a rationale is recorded. Until there is a current-source receipt confirming that each relationship remains factually accurate, the completeness and `risk` of this answer remain `unknown`.

27(22px 어긋남). **더 강한 사실이 대체했다** — 드릴인하면 LNB 180px 가 통째로 사라지고 878px 을 802px 한 칼럼이 쓴다. 그 자리는 이미 비어 있었다.", "+", "+**교훈**: 소유자의 감각어(\"AI 같다\")를 지문으로 번역하는 것은 chief 의 일이 맞다. 다만 그 번역은 **가설이지 판정이 아니다** — 자리가 실측으로 기각할 수 있게 \"틀리면 정정하라\"를 명시적으로 붙여야 한다. 이번엔 붙였고 둘이 기각됐다. 안 붙였으면 chief 의 오독이 처방이 됐다.", "+", "+---", "+", "+### 기록된 반대 (반증 조건 포함)", "+", "+**1. 위계석 — 채움 해제가 틀릴 수 있다.**", "+> *\"Claude Code 를 채운 건 위계 오류가 아니라 옳은 베팅이다. 사용자 대다수가 Claude Code 라면 70% 를 맞히는 기본값이 모두에게 읽기를 시키는 동등한 넷보다 낫다.\"*", "+**반증 조건**: 5초 노출 3인 테스트 또는 실사용에서 **Claude Code 사용자 비율 ≥70%** 가 관측되면 채움이 자기 값을 한다. **그때의 처방은 「넷 다 평평」으로 되돌리는 것이 docs/benchmark/results/2026-08-25-codex-B1-on.txt:1842:./src/entities/docs-vault/data/content.json:3:  "AGENT-MEMORY-POSITIONING": "# Agent Memory Positioning\n\n> Current product framing for launch, README copy, and prioritization.\n> Last updated: 2026-06-21.\n\n## One-line Position\n\n**A repo-native memory layer for Claude Code, Cursor, and Codex.**\n\nontology-atlas keeps a local, git-backed mental model of a codebase that AI\ncoding agents can read, query, and maintain through MCP.\n\n## The User Problem\n\nAI coding agents are useful, but they forget the durable structure of a\ncodebase between sessions:\n\n- which domains and capabilities exist\n- which files implement which capability\n- which dependencies matter before changing a module\n- which architectural decisions were already discovered\n- which documentation is canonical and which is stale\n\nWithout a shared memory layer, every agent session starts by re-discovering the\nsame project shape from source files and chat history.\n\nThe minimum supported path is intentionally plain: connect Atlas MCP or run the\nAtlas CLI from Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, or another coding agent, and the agent\nshould already receive a useful workspace brief, graph health check, handoff\npacket, and reviewable memory-diff workflow. No CodeGraph, Serena, language\nserver, grep wrapper, or external source-index service is required for the\nproduct to be usable.\n\nAtlas should not promise to replace code-reading tools. Built-in source search,\ngrep, language servers, Serena, CodeGraph, AST indexes, and similar tools are\ngood at structural facts: definitions, callers, imports, routes, and local\nimpact paths. Atlas helps the coding agent one layer above that: it preserves\nthe task starting point, product meaning, capability boundary, implementation\nevidence, and validation path that explain why those code facts matter.\n\n## Product Bet\n\nThe product is not valuable because it is \"an ontology editor.\" Developers do\nnot want another system they must manually maintain.\n\nThe product is valuable if it reduces the maintenance cost of AI-agent memory:\n\n1. Open a repo and generate an ontology draft automatically.\n2. Let the agent propose mental-model changes after real code work.\n3. Let the developer review those changes like a git diff.\n4. Make the next agent session visibly better because the memory is already in\n   the repo.\n\nThat loop is the product. The ontology graph is the implementation substrate.\n\nThe coding-agent value is therefore not \"Atlas reads all code for you.\" The\nvalue is \"Atlas tells the agent what to inspect, why it matters, and what must\nbe verified before acting.\" Store meaningful implementation evidence as\n`element` nodes and relations; leave exhaustive symbol graphs to deterministic\ncode-intelligence tools.\n\n## Why This Can Matter\n\nThe strongest positioning is:\n\n> Your AI coding agent forgets your codebase. Give it a local, git-backed memory\n> it can read and maintain.\n\nThis is stronger than:\n\n> Local-first ontology graph workbench with deterministic compiler and MCP tools.\n\nThe second sentence is technically accurate, but it leads with implementation\nlanguage. The first sentence leads with the daily pain of AI-assisted coding.\n\n## Success Conditions\n\nontology-atlas becomes a real product if a new repo can show value in one short\nloop:\n\n```text\ninit -> bootstrap -> agent answers better through MCP -> agent proposes sync\n-> developer reviews diff -> next task benefits\n```\n\nTarget: the first visible value should appear within 10 minutes.\n\n## Failure Conditions\n\nThe product fails if users feel they must become ontology maintainers before\nthey get value.\n\nRisk signals:\n\n- onboarding starts with manual node authoring\n- the hosted website feels like the primary product instead of intro/download\n- agent sync is not visible in git diffs\n- `health`, `workspace_brief`, and `maintenance_plan` explain problems but do\n  not lead to obvious next actions\n- bootstrap produces a graph that looks impressive but does not improve the\n  next agent interaction\n\n## What The Recent Engine Work Enables\n\nThe recent compiler/MCP/CLI work is meaningful because it supports the memory\nloop:\n\n- `compile_ontology` turns markdown frontmatter into a deterministic graph\n  artifact with `graphHash`, issue counts, aliases, pagination, and optional\n  indexes.\n- `query_ontology` lets agents ask graph-database-like questions without\n  pulling the whole vault into context.\n- `health` and `workspace_brief` give first-contact diagnosis before an agent\n  starts editing.\n- `maintenance_plan` turns graph cleanup into an ordered work queue.\n- Structured MCP schemas and error codes let agents recover from bad calls\n  instead of parsing fragile prose.\n- `analyze_repo_structure` and `infer_imports` reduce the cost of creating the\n  first draft from code.\n\nThis is the difference between a markdown graph toy and an agent-usable memory\nworkbench.\n\n## Agent Builder Concerns Lens\n\nUse this lens when deciding whether a new Ontology Atlas feature is worth adding.\nThe point is not to copy any agent framework UI. The point is to answer the\noperational problems that experienced agent builders keep designing around.\n\nCurrent research scan, 2026-06-05:\n\n- Anthropic's agent guidance stresses that simple workflows should stay simple,\n  while true agents should be reserved for open-ended loops that need tools and\n  environmental feedback:\n  <https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-effective-agents>\n- Claude Code guidance repeatedly treats context as the scarce resource:\n  project memory files, subagents, skills, and task-focused sessions exist to\n  keep the main agent context clean and durable:\n  <https://code.claude.com/docs/en/best-practices>\n  <https://support.claude.com/en/articles/14553240-give-claude-context-claude-md-and-better-prompts>\n  <https://support.claude.com/en/articles/14554000-claude-code-power-user-tips>\n- OpenAI's Codex and Agents SDK guidance puts persistent repo instructions,\n  issue-shaped prompts, MCP connections, approvals, and human-in-the-loop\n  resumption at the center of agent development:\n  <https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/6a2631dc-783e-479b-b1a4-af0cfbd38630/how-openai-uses-codex.pdf>\n  <https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-js/guides/mcp/>\n- Cognition's agent verification writing frames async coding-agent output as\n  incomplete until it includes end-to-end proof artifacts such as labeled\n  screenshots, videos, and pass/fail assertions:\n  <https://cognition.ai/blog/testing-development>\n- Google ADK frames agent infrastructure around structured context, sessions,\n  memory, tools, callbacks, traces, failures, and resumability:\n  <https://adk.dev/>\n- LangChain's memory work separates semantic, episodic, and procedural memory\n  so agents can distinguish durable facts, prior episodes, and reusable skills:\n  <https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/deepagents/memory>\n- MCP frames agent integration as a typed boundary for tools, resources, and\n  prompts rather than an ad hoc pile of API calls:\n  <https://modelcontextprotocol.io/>\n\nTranslate those concerns into Atlas product tests:\n\n1. **Context selection**: Does the feature help an agent get the smallest useful\n   packet instead of flooding the context window?\n2. **Durable memory**: Does it turn an agent discovery into repo-backed markdown\n   that the next Claude Code, Codex, or Cursor session can reuse?\n3. **Tool and relation clarity**: Does it expose which MCP tool, graph query,\n   node slug, relation type, or source file the agent should use next?\n4. **Traceability**: Can a human see what changed, why it changed, and which\n   graph proof or validation command supports it?\n5. **Guardrails**: Does it fail closed before risky writes, stale assumptions,\n   ambiguous relations, or unverified graph rows become committed memory?\n6. **Human-in-the-loop economy**: Does it ask the developer for review at the\n   right point, not on every low-value observation?\n7. **Resumability**: If a session stops, can another agent reconstruct the\n   working state from the vault, git diff, graph health, and saved briefs?\n\nNear-term product implications:\n\n- Prefer \"agent-ready packets\" over long explanations: selected node profile,\n  blast radius, relation preflight, source file evidence, and next command in\n  one copyable bundle.\n- Make MCP connection and graph health visible as runtime state, not hidden in\n  documentation.\n- Treat draft ontology changes as first-class: staged, reviewable, discardable,\n  and backed by validation before they become durable memory.\n- Keep the visual UI quiet by default, but make proof, trace, and handoff\n  available through popovers, collapsible panels, and copy actions.\n- When adding a feature, ask whether it makes the next agent session cheaper,\n  safer, or more accurate. If not, it is probably decorative.\n\n## Positioning Guardrail\n\nUse \"ontology\" in technical documentation and architecture. Use \"repo-native\nmemory layer\" in launch copy and first-contact README text.\n\nThe marketable promise is not that users can draw a better graph. The promise is\nthat their AI coding agent stops starting from zero.\n\nWhen explaining the code side, use this boundary:\n\n```text\nBuilt-in search, grep, language servers, Serena, CodeGraph, and source indexes\nfind code structure.\nOntology Atlas preserves product/system meaning, implementation evidence, and\nthe next verification path for humans and AI agents.\n```\n",
